Semi-global Kuranishi charts and the definition of contact homology Thanks: KH supported by NSF Grants DMS-1406564 and DMS-1549147.
Erkao Bao, Ko Honda

Abstract
. We define the contact homology algebra for any contact manifold and show that it is an invariant of the contact manifold. More precisely, given a contact manifold ( M , ξ ) (M,\xi) and some auxiliary data 𝒟 \mathcal{D} , we define an algebra H ​ C ​ ( 𝒟 ) HC(\mathcal{D}) . If 𝒟 1 \mathcal{D}_{1} and 𝒟 2 \mathcal{D}_{2} are two choices of auxiliary data for ( M , ξ ) (M,\xi) , then H ​ C ​ ( 𝒟 1 ) HC(\mathcal{D}_{1}) and H ​ C ​ ( 𝒟 2 ) HC(\mathcal{D}_{2}) are isomorphic. We use a simplified version of Kuranishi perturbation theory, consisting of semi-global Kuranishi charts .

Primary 53D10; Secondary 53D40.

1.
Introduction

Symplectic field theory (SFT), proposed about 20 years ago by Eliashberg-Givental-Hofer
[
EGH
]
, is a package which gives invariants of contact manifolds and symplectic manifolds with boundary as well as gluing formulas for Gromov-Witten invariants on closed symplectic manifolds. The transversality theory in the somewhere injective case and the Fredholm theory were carried out by Dragnev
[
Dr
]
and the SFT compactness was worked out by
[
BEHWZ
]
, both very early on in the development of the theory. However, the rigorous foundations of the full theory are still under development, although parts of the theory are gradually becoming more rigorous, thanks to the efforts of many authors, primarily by Hofer-Wysocki-Zehnder
[
HWZ3
]
. Partial work has been done by Hutchings-Nelson
[
HN
]
and Bao-Honda
[
BH
]
for cylindrical contact homology in dimension three and Bourgeois-Oancea
[
BO
]
on
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic homology. Also the Kuranishi perturbation theory of Fukaya-Ono
[
FO
]
and Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono
[
FO3
]
(which is closest in spirit to this work) and the approaches of Liu-Tian
[
LT
]
and
[
Ru
]
may be used to define SFT, although this has not been done yet.

The goal of this paper is to make a further contribution to this effort and define the full contact homology differential graded algebra (dga) for any closed contact manifold in any dimension and show that its isomorphism class is an invariant of the contact manifold. More precisely, given a closed cooriented contact
(2n+1)
-dimensional manifold
(M,\xi)
and some auxiliary data
\mathcal{D}
which includes a nondegenerate contact form
\alpha
for
\xi
, we define a direct limit dga
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{D})
whose homology is called the
full contact homology algebra

HC(\mathcal{D})
. We then prove that:

Theorem 1.0.1
.

If
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are two choices of auxiliary data for
(M,\xi)
, then
HC(\mathcal{D}_{1})
and
HC(\mathcal{D}_{2})
are isomorphic. Hence the isomorphism class of the algebra
HC(\mathcal{D})
is an invariant of
(M,\xi)
.

We denote the isomorphism class of
HC(\mathcal{D})
by
HC(\xi)
. We also prove the following:

Theorem 1.0.2
.

Let
(W,\alpha)
be a compact Liouville cobordism which restricts to
(M_{+},\xi_{+}=\ker\alpha|_{M_{+}})
at the positive boundary and to
(M_{-},\xi_{-}=\ker\alpha|_{M_{-}})
at the negative boundary. Then there is an algebra homomorphism

\Phi_{*}:HC(\xi_{+})\to HC(\xi_{-}).

In the case when
\xi_{+}=\xi_{-}
and
(W,\alpha)
is the trivial cobordism, we have
\Phi_{*}=\operatorname{id}
.
1
1

1

We will often suppress the
\alpha
from the notation of a Liouville cobordism.

The construction of the direct limit dga
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{D})
and the proofs of Theorems
1.0.1
and
1.0.2
use a particularly simple version of the Kuranishi multivalued perturbation theory of
[
FO
,
FO3
]
that we call
semi-global Kuranishi structures
. The perturbations that we use are supported near the ends of finite energy
J
-holomorphic curves and are described using the asymptotic eigenfunctions of the asymptotic operators. Let
\widehat{W}
be a completed Liouville cobordism,

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

be the moduli space of Fredholm index
\operatorname{ind}=k

J
-holomorphic maps
\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
that are asymptotic to collections
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
of Reeb orbits at the positive and negative ends, and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
be the space of SFT buildings in
\widehat{W}
of total Fredholm index
\operatorname{ind}=k
that are asymptotic to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
at the positive and negative ends and whose domains, when glued up, have topological type
\dot{F}
. If
\overline{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{M}
, then
we only need one Kuranishi chart, which we call a semi-global Kuranishi chart
.

Remark 1.0.3
.

Throughout this paper we will freely use the SFT compactness theorem of
[
BEHWZ
]
without explicit mention.

Remark 1.0.4
.

We emphasize that our simplification
crucially uses the existence of at least one end limiting to a Reeb orbit
(See Section
4.2.5
) and can be used to define SFT in the case when
\widehat{W}
is an exact symplectic cobordism, although we only deal with contact homology in this paper. Our simplified methods are not readily applicable to the case when there is a
closed
multiply-covered
J
-holomorphic curve. However, we expect the combination of our methods and those of
[
FO
]
and
[
FO3
]
to yield a rigorous definition of SFT.

Warning 1.0.5
.

In this paper we do
not
prove that:

(1)

dga morphisms induced by homotopic Liouville cobordisms are dga homotopic (for any definition of dga homotopy);

(2)

the set of linearized contact homologies over the set of all augmentations of
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{D})
is an invariant.

Finally, during the preparation of this paper, Pardon
[
Pa
]
posted a paper proving the existence and invariance of contact homology in arbitrary dimensions.

Outline of the paper.
We will be using the language of orbifolds and multisections, following Adem-Leida-Ruan
[
ALR
]
and Fukaya-Ono
[
FO
]
; this is reviewed in Section
2
. After some preparation in Sections
3
and
4
, we construct the interior semi-global Kuranishi chart in Section
5
, with some modifications in Section
8.4
. The semi-global Kuranishi structures are constructed in Section
8
, using the gluing results which are stated in Section
6
; they are average specimens of the “gluing theorem” type and are proved in Section
7
. Once the semi-global Kuranishi structures are constructed, the definition of contact homology, the definition of chain maps, and the invariance of contact homology (Theorems
1.0.1
and
1.0.2
) follow the usual lines of argument and are carried out in Section
9
.

We note that the discussion until the end of Section
6
is valid for curves of any genus; starting from Section
8
we specialize to contact homology.

Acknowledgements.
We thank Francis Bonahon, Tobias Ekholm, Michael Hutchings, Eleny Ionel, Gang Liu, Kaoru Ono, Russell Avdek, and Garrett Alston for very helpful discussions. We also thank Tobias Ekholm, John Pardon, and Russell Avdek for pointing out some errors, Kenji Fukaya for suggesting that we instead use the
\alpha
-action to define
I_{\gamma}
in Section
4.2.4
, and the anonymous referees for extensive lists of comments.

2.
Orbifolds, orbibundles, and multisections

In this section we review the basics of (effective) orbifolds, orbibundles, and multisections from
[
ALR
]
,
[
FO
]
,
[
FT
]
, and
[
FO33
]
. The definitions in this section, while elementary, are a bit cumbersome to state. Also, at the end of the day, all we do is replace the words “manifold”, “vector bundle”, and “section” by “orbifold”, “orbibundle”, and “multisection”, and treat them in exactly the same way for our purposes. For the above reasons the reader may want to skip this section and return to it as the relevant concepts gradually start appearing in the rest of the paper.

In this paper all our orbifolds are effective, unless stated otherwise.

2.1.
Orbifolds and orbibundles

We state the definitions in the smooth category but they can easily be adapted to the
C^{k}
-category.

Definition 2.1.1

(Orbifold charts)
.

Given a topological space
\mathsf{X}
, a triple
(V,\Gamma,\phi)
is an
orbifold chart of
\mathsf{X}
if

(1)

V
is a connected open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
;

(2)

\Gamma
is a finite group that acts smoothly and effectively on
V
; and

(3)

\phi:V\to\mathsf{X}
is a
\Gamma
-invariant continuous map such that the quotient map
V/\Gamma\to\mathsf{X}
, and is homeomorphism onto an open subset
U
of
\mathsf{X}
.

We will often abuse notation and view
V/\Gamma
as an open subset of
\mathsf{X}
.
If the “effective” condition is not satisfied in (2), then we have a
non-effective
orbifold chart.

Definition 2.1.2

(Embedding of orbifold charts)
.

If
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
and
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
are orbifold charts of
\mathsf{X}
, we say that
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})

embeds into
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
if there exists a smooth embedding
\psi_{ji}:V_{i}\hookrightarrow V_{j}
such that
\phi_{j}\circ\psi_{ji}=\phi_{i}
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

If
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
embeds into
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
, then there exists an injective group homomorphism
\theta_{ji}:\Gamma_{i}\to\Gamma_{j}
(see
[
ALR
]
for details) such that

(1)

\psi_{ji}
is
\theta_{ji}
-equivariant,

(2)

\theta_{ii}=\operatorname{id}
, and

(3)

\theta_{kj}\circ\theta_{ji}=\theta_{ki}
, if
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
embeds into
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
and
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
embeds into
(V_{k},\Gamma_{k},\phi_{k})
.

Definition 2.1.4

(Orbifolds)
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a Hausdorff, second countable topological space. An
(smooth) orbifold atlas of
\mathsf{X}
is a family
\mathcal{O}=\{(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of orbifold charts such that:

(1)

\{U_{i}=V_{i}/\Gamma_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
forms an open cover of
\mathsf{X}
; and

(2)

for any
x\in U_{i}\cap U_{j}\subseteq\mathsf{X}
, there exist a neighborhood
U_{k}\subseteq U_{i}\cap U_{j}
of
x
, a chart
(V_{k},\Gamma_{k},\phi_{k})
for
U_{k}
, and embeddings of orbifold charts
\psi_{ik}:(V_{k},\Gamma_{k},\phi_{k})\hookrightarrow(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
and
\psi_{jk}:(V_{k},\Gamma_{k},\phi_{k})\hookrightarrow(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
.

An orbifold atlas
\mathcal{O}

refines
another atlas
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
if for every chart in
\mathcal{O}
there exists an embedding into some chart of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
. Two orbifold atlases are
equivalent
if they have a common refinement. We denote by
[\mathcal{O}]
the equivalence class of
\mathcal{O}
and
call the pair
\mathbb{X}=(\mathsf{X},[\mathcal{O}])
a
smooth (effective) orbifold
.

Notation 2.1.5
.

We sometimes abuse notation and write
x\in\mathbb{X}
instead of
x\in\mathsf{X}
.

For any
i,j\in\mathcal{I}
, we denote
i\geq j
, if
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
embeds into
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
.
For any
x\in\mathsf{X}
, the
stabilizer

\Gamma^{x}
of
x
is the inverse limit of
(\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{x}},\{\theta_{ij}\}_{i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{x}})
, where
\mathcal{I}_{x}=\{i\in\mathcal{I}~|~x\in\operatorname{Im}\phi_{i}\}.

Then for any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{x}
, we have an injective map
\Theta_{i}^{x}:\Gamma^{x}\to\Gamma_{i}.

Since the
\Gamma_{i}
are finite groups, for sufficiently large
i
,
\Theta_{i}^{x}
are isomorphisms.

Definition 2.1.6

(Orbifold maps)
.

A
(smooth) orbifold map

\lambda:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},[\mathcal{O}^{\prime}])\to\mathbb{X}=(\mathsf{X},[\mathcal{O}])

consists of

(1)

an underlying continuous map
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{X}
, usually denoted by
\lambda
by abuse of notation,

such that:

(2)

there exist orbifold atlases
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{O}
for
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{X}
, so that for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, we have charts
(V^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
around
x^{\prime}
and
(V,\Gamma,\phi)\in\mathcal{O}
around
\lambda(x^{\prime})
such that
\lambda(V^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime})\subset V/\Gamma
, a homomorphism
\rho:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
, and a
\rho
-equivariant smooth lift
\widehat{\lambda}:V^{\prime}\to V
of
\lambda|_{V^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}}
such that
\phi^{\prime}\circ\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda\circ\phi
.

For any
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, the orbifold map
\lambda:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
induces a homomorphism
\lambda^{x^{\prime}}:\Gamma^{\prime x^{\prime}}\to\Gamma^{\lambda(x^{\prime})}
between the stabilizers.

Definition 2.1.7

(Orbifold embedding)
.

A (smooth) orbifold map
\lambda:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is a
(smooth) embedding
if

(1)

\lambda:\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{X}
is a homeomorphism onto its image;

(2)

\lambda^{x^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
; and

(3)

Definition
2.1.6
(2) holds with the additional requirements

(a)

\widehat{\lambda}
is an embedding, and

(b)

\Gamma^{\prime}\simeq\Gamma^{\prime x^{\prime}}
and
\Gamma\simeq\Gamma^{\lambda(x^{\prime})}
.

Definition 2.1.8

(Composition of orbifold maps)
.

Given orbifold maps

\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime},[\mathcal{O}^{\prime\prime}])\xrightarrow{\mu}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},[\mathcal{O}^{\prime}])\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbb{X}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},[\mathcal{O}^{\prime}])\xrightarrow{\lambda}\mathbb{X}=(\mathsf{X},[\mathcal{O}]),

their composition
\lambda\circ\mu
is given as follows:

(1)

the underlying continuous map
\lambda\circ\mu:\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathsf{X}
is the composition of the underlying continuous maps; and

(2)

Condition (2) in Definition
2.1.6
is satisfied for the composition
\lambda\circ\mu
since Condition (2) holds for both
\lambda
and
\mu
and the equivariant smooth lifts
\widehat{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\mu}
can be composed.

To pull back orbibundles in general, one requires the maps between orbifolds to be strong maps in
[
MP
]
or (equivalently) good maps in
[
CR
]
instead of just smooth maps as above.

Definition 2.1.9

(Good maps
[
CR
]
)
.

An orbifold map

\lambda:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},[\mathcal{O}^{\prime}])\to\mathbb{X}=(\mathsf{X},[\mathcal{O}])

is called a
good map
if for any orbifold atlas
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
of
\mathsf{X}
there exist an orbifold atlas
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, a refinement
\mathcal{O}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
, and a map
i:\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{O}
such that:

(1)

for any orbifold chart
o^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
\lambda
maps
o^{\prime}
to
i(o^{\prime})
so that Condition (2) in Definition
2.1.6
is satisfied;

(2)

for any two orbifold charts
o^{\prime}_{1},o^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
, if
o^{\prime}_{1}
embeds into
o^{\prime}_{2}
, then
i(o^{\prime}_{1})
embeds into
i(o^{\prime}_{2})
.

Example 2.1.10
.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{1}
and
\mathbb{X}_{2}
be orbifolds. The product
\mathbb{X}_{1}\times\mathbb{X}_{2}
has an obvious orbifold structure. The projection map
\operatorname{pr}_{i}:\mathbb{X}_{1}\times\mathbb{X}_{2}\to\mathbb{X}_{i}
for
i=1,2
is a good map.

Example 2.1.11
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an orbifold, and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
be an open subset of
\mathsf{X}
. There is an obvious orbifold structure on
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
denoted by
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
. Then the inclusion map
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is a good map.

Definition 2.1.12

(Orbibundle charts)
.

Let
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
be a continuous map between topological spaces. The triple
(V\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma,\widetilde{\phi})
is an
orbibundle chart of
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
of rank
n
over
(V,\Gamma,\phi)
,
if:
2
2

2

We will often suppress
(V,\Gamma,\phi)
from the notation.

(1)

(V,\Gamma,\phi)
is an (effective) orbifold chart of
\mathsf{X}
;

(2)

\Gamma
acts diagonally on
V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a linear action on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
;

(3)

\widetilde{\phi}:V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathsf{E}
is a
\Gamma
-invariant continuous map such that the quotient map is a homeomorphism
(V\times\mathbb{R}^{n})/\Gamma\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi^{-1}(\phi(V))
; and

(4)

\pi\circ\widetilde{\phi}=\phi\circ\operatorname{pr}_{V}
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{V}:V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to V
is the projection onto the first factor.

A
local orbibundle model
is a triple
(V\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma,\operatorname{pr}_{V})
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{V}
is often suppressed from the notation.

Definition 2.1.13

(Embedding of orbibundle charts)
.

If

(V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})\quad\mbox{and}\quad(V_{j}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{j},\widetilde{\phi}_{j})

are orbibundle charts of
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
of rank
n
over
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
and
(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j})
, we say that
(V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})

embeds into
(V_{j}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{j},\widetilde{\phi}_{j})
if there exist
embeddings of orbifold charts

\displaystyle\psi_{ji}:(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})\to(V_{j},\Gamma_{j},\phi_{j}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}:(V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})\to(V_{j}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{j},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}),

such that

\operatorname{pr}_{V_{j}}\circ\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}=\psi_{ji}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{V_{i}}

and
\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}
is a linear isomorphism on each fiber. We often refer to the embedding
\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}
with the understanding that
\psi_{ji}
is part of the data.

Definition 2.1.14

(Orbibundles)
.

Let
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
be a continuous map between Hausdorff, second countable topological spaces.
A
(smooth) rank
n
orbibundle atlas of
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
is a family
\mathcal{Y}=\{(V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of rank
n
orbibundle charts of
E
over
\mathcal{O}=\{(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
such that:

(1)

\{U_{i}=V_{i}/\Gamma_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
forms an open cover of
\mathsf{X}
; and

(2)

for any
x\in U_{i}\cap U_{j}
where
U_{i}=V_{i}/\Gamma_{i}
and
U_{j}=V_{j}/\Gamma_{j}
, there exist a neighborhood
U_{k}\subseteq U_{i}\cap U_{j}
of
x
, an orbibundle chart
(V_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{k},\widetilde{\phi}_{k})
such that
U_{k}=V_{k}/\Gamma_{k}
, and embeddings of orbibundle charts

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}_{ik}:(V_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{k},\widetilde{\phi}_{k})\hookrightarrow(V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}_{jk}:(V_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{k},\widetilde{\phi}_{k})\hookrightarrow(V_{j}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma_{j},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}).

Let
[\mathcal{Y}]
be the equivalence class of the orbibundle atlas
\mathcal{Y}
under refinement.

The triple
(\mathbb{E}=(\mathsf{E},[\mathcal{Y}]),\mathbb{X}=(\mathsf{X},[\mathcal{O}]),\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X})
, where
\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
is a smooth orbifold map and
\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{O}
form an orbibundle atlas of the underlying continuous map
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{X}
, is an
orbibundle
. We refer to
\mathbb{E}
as the
total space
and
\mathbb{X}
as the
base
of the orbibundle.

Remark 2.1.15
.

In practice, our orbifolds and orbibundles will be particularly simple and can be described by a single single orbifold or orbibundle chart, i.e., are
global quotient
orbifolds or orbibundles.

Definition 2.1.16

(Orbibundle maps)
.

A
(smooth) orbibundle map

f=(f^{\sharp},f^{\flat}):(\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}:\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{X},\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X})

between orbibundles consists of orbifold maps
f^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{E}
and
f^{\flat}:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
such that

(1)

the underlying continuous maps
f^{\sharp}:\mathsf{E}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{E}
and
f^{\flat}:\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{X}
satisfy
f^{\flat}\circ\pi^{\prime}=\pi\circ f^{\sharp}
,

(2)

there exist orbibundle atlases
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
for
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Y}
for
\mathbb{E}
, orbibundle charts
(V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
and
(V\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma,\widetilde{\phi})\in\mathcal{Y}
with
x^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}(V^{\prime})
and
f^{\flat}(x^{\prime})\in\phi(V)
for each
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, and equivariant smooth lifts

\widehat{f}^{\flat}:V^{\prime}\to V,\quad\widehat{f}^{\sharp}:V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\to V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that

\operatorname{pr}_{V}\circ\widehat{f}^{\sharp}=\widehat{f}^{\flat}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{V^{\prime}},\quad f^{\flat}\circ\phi^{\prime}=\phi\circ\widehat{f}^{\flat},\quad f^{\sharp}\circ\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\phi}\circ\widehat{f}^{\sharp},

and for any
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
the map
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\circ\widehat{f}^{\sharp}(v^{\prime},\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is linear. (Here
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the projection onto the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
factor.)

Definition 2.1.17
.

A (smooth) orbibundle map

f=(f^{\sharp},f^{\flat}):(\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}:\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{X},\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X})

is a
(smooth) orbibundle embedding
if

(1)

f^{\sharp}
and
f^{\flat}
are homeomorphisms onto their images;

(2)

the induced morphisms
(f^{\sharp})^{e^{\prime}}
and
(f^{\flat})^{\pi^{\prime}(e^{\prime})}
between the stabilizers are isomorphisms for any
e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}^{\prime}
; and

(3)

Definition
2.1.16
(2) holds with the following additional requirements:

(a)

\widehat{f}^{\sharp}
and
\widehat{f}^{\flat}
are embeddings, and

(b)

\Gamma^{\prime}\simeq\Gamma^{\prime x^{\prime}}
and
\Gamma\simeq\Gamma^{f^{\flat}(x^{\prime})}
.

Fact 2.1.18
.

Given an orbibundle
\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
and a good map
\lambda:\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, there exists a naturally defined pullback orbibundle
\pi^{\prime}:\lambda^{*}\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and an orbibundle map

(\pi^{\prime}:\lambda^{*}\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\to(\pi:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}).

2.2.
Multisections

Given a topological space
W
, we denote its
n
-fold symmetric product by

\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(W):=W^{n}/S_{n}.

Here
S_{n}
is the symmetric group of
n
elements which acts on
W^{n}
by

\sigma(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(n)}),

where
\sigma\in S_{n}
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in W^{n}
.
We denote the equivalence class of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in
\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(W)
by
[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
.
If a group
\Gamma
acts on
W
, then
\Gamma
also acts on
\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(W)
diagonally by

g[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]=[g(x_{1}),\dots,g(x_{n})],

where
g\in\Gamma
and
[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\in\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(W)
.
There is an inclusion

\iota^{n}:\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(W)\to\operatorname{Sym}^{mn}(W),

[w_{1},\dots,w_{m}]\mapsto[\underbrace{w_{1},\dots,w_{1}}_{n\text{ copies}},\dots,\underbrace{w_{m},\dots,w_{m}}_{n\text{ copies}}].

Let
(V\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\Gamma)
be a local orbibundle model of rank
n
.

For convenience, we write
L=V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and use the notation
(L\to V,\Gamma)
instead for the local model.

Let
\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(L)
be the fiberwise symmetric product, i.e.,
\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(L)=\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times V
.
Then
\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(L)\to V
is a fiber bundle with an equivariant
\Gamma
-action.

Definition 2.2.1

(Multisections of a local model)
.

A
degree
m
multisection (or
m
-multisection) of the local orbibundle model
(L\to V,\Gamma)

is a
\Gamma
-equivariant section of
\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(L)\to V
. A
section
is a degree
1
multisection.

Definition 2.2.2
.

(1)

An
m
-multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
is
liftable
if there exists a section
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m}):V\to L^{m}
which is not necessarily
\Gamma
-equivariant, such that
\mathbf{s}=p\circ\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}
, where
L^{m}=\mathbb{R}^{mn}\times V
and
p:L^{m}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(L)
is the quotient map. We call each such
s_{i}
a
branch of
\mathbf{s}
.

(2)

A choice of
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}
is a
lifting
of
\mathbf{s}
; a multisection with a choice of lifting is said to be
lifted
. Given
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m})
, its
n
-fold splitting is

\iota^{n}\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}=(\underbrace{s_{1},\dots,s_{1}}_{n\text{ copies}},\dots,\underbrace{s_{m},\dots,s_{m}}_{n\text{ copies}}).

We assign the weight
\frac{1}{m}
to each branch
s_{i}
of
{\bf s}
, if
{\bf s}
is a degree
m
multisection.

Definition 2.2.3
.

A liftable
m
-multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
and a liftable
n
-multisection
\mathbf{t}
of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
are
transverse
(denoted by
\mathbf{s}\pitchfork\mathbf{t}
) if
s_{i}
is transverse to
t_{j}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
.

In the above definition, we also refer to
s_{i}^{-1}(t_{j})\subset V
, where
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, as a
branch
, and assign the weight
\frac{1}{mn}
to it.
We denote by

\mathbf{s}^{-1}(\mathbf{t}):=\cup_{1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n}s_{i}^{-1}(t_{j})

the weighted space (i.e., a space together with a map to
\mathbb{Q}^{\geq 0}
) where the weight of each point is defined by summing up the weights of all branches containing it.

An
m
-multisection of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
can be mapped to an
\ell m
-multisection of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
by postcomposing with
\iota^{\ell}
, which is interpreted as acting on each fiber separately.

Definition 2.2.4
.

An
\ell
-multisection
\mathbf{s}
and an
m
-multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
of
(L\to V,\Gamma)
are said to be
equivalent
if
\iota^{m}\circ\mathbf{s}=\iota^{\ell}\circ\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
.

If
{\bf s}
and
{\bf s}^{\prime}
are equivalent, then
\mathbf{s}^{-1}(\mathbf{t})
and
\mathbf{s^{\prime}}^{-1}(\mathbf{t})
represent the same weighted space.

Definition 2.2.5

(Multisections of an orbibundle)
.

A
multisection
\mathbf{s}
of an orbibundle
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
is a family
\{(L_{i},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
over an orbibundle atlas indexed by
\mathcal{I}
such that

(1)

L_{i}=V_{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\{(L_{i},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an orbibundle atlas of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
;

(2)

\mathbf{s}_{i}
is an
m_{i}
-multisection of
(L_{i}\to V_{i},\Gamma_{i})
;

(3)

for any embedding

\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}:(L_{i},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})\hookrightarrow(L_{j},\Gamma_{j},\widetilde{\phi}_{j})

of orbibundle charts, the pullback
m_{j}
-multisection

\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{i}:=({\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}}^{-1})^{m_{j}}\circ\mathbf{s}_{j}\circ\psi_{ji}

and the
m_{i}
-multisection
\mathbf{s}_{i}
of
(L_{i}\to V_{i},\Gamma_{i})
are equivalent. Here

\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}^{m_{j}}:\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(L_{i})\to\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(L_{j}),

({\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}}^{-1})^{m_{j}}:\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(L_{j})|_{{\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}}^{m_{j}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(L_{i}))}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(L_{i})

are the obvious maps induced by

\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}:L_{i}\to L_{j}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}^{-1}:L_{j}|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}(L_{i})}\to L_{i}.

A
section
\mathbf{s}
is a multisection which has degree
1
on each local orbibundle model.

The multisection
{\bf s}
is
liftable
if each
{\bf s_{i}}
is liftable.

The multisection
{\bf s}
is
lifted
if there exist an orbibundle atlas (still indexed by
\mathcal{I}
) and a lifting
\widetilde{\bf s}_{i}
of each
{\bf s}_{i}
such that

\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}_{i}:={\widetilde{\psi}_{ji}}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}_{j}\circ\psi_{ji}

and
\iota^{m_{j}/m_{i}}\widetilde{\bf s}_{i}
agree as unordered sets with multiplicities.

In this paper, we restrict ourselves to liftable or lifted multisections.

To better describe the intersections of multisections of orbifolds and semi-global Kuranishi structures, we introduce the notion of a weighted branched manifold. Roughly speaking, a branched manifold is a complex embedded in a Euclidean space such that at each point there is a unique tangent space. For convenience we also enlarge the category of underlying objects to include images of immersions, and not just embeddings. Intuitively, it is what
\mathbf{s}^{-1}(\mathbf{t})
should be, if
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
are multisections of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathbf{s}\pitchfork\mathbf{t}
.

Definition 2.2.6
.

A
weighted branched manifold structure of dimension
n
with weight
w
on a Hausdorff, second countable topological space
Z
is a pair

(\mathfrak{F}=\{(U_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{i},\Gamma_{i})~|~i\in\mathcal{I}\},w)

such that:

(1)

w:Z\to\mathbb{Q}^{\geq 0}
is a continuous function;

(2)

\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an open cover of
Z
;

(3)

\Gamma_{i}
is a group which acts effectively on
U_{i}
;

(4)

\mathfrak{B}_{i}=\{(Y_{i}^{j},\varphi_{i}^{j},w_{i}^{j})~|~j\in\mathcal{J}_{i}\}
with
\mathcal{J}_{i}
finite, where:

(a)

Y_{i}^{j}
is a connected, relatively closed subset of
U_{i}
and
\cup_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{i}}Y_{i}^{j}=U_{i}
;

(b)

\varphi_{i}^{j}:Y_{i}^{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a homeomorphism onto some open disk;

(c)

\varphi_{i}^{j}\circ(\varphi_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})^{-1}
, where defined, is smooth for any
i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, and
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{i^{\prime}}
;

(d)

w_{i}^{j}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\geq 0}
and for any
z\in U_{i}
,
w(z)=\textstyle\sum w_{i}^{j}
,
where the sum is taken over all
j\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
such that
z\in Y_{i}^{j}
;

(e)

\Gamma_{i}
permutes the set
\{Y_{i}^{j}~|~j\in\mathcal{J}_{i}\}
and
w_{i}^{j}=w_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
if
\Gamma_{i}
maps
Y_{i}^{j}
to
Y_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
(in particular,
w|_{U_{i}}
is
\Gamma_{i}
-invariant); and

(5)

\mathfrak{F}
is a maximal collection satisfing (2)–(4).

On each orbibundle chart
(L_{i}\to V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})
of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
over
(V_{i},\Gamma_{i},\phi_{i})
,
if
\mathbf{s}_{i}
is an
m_{i}
-multisection and
\mathbf{t}_{i}
is an
n_{i}
-multisection,
then we define

Y_{i}^{j}=\phi_{i}(s_{\ell}^{-1}(t_{k})),\quad U_{i}:=\cup_{1\leq\ell\leq m_{i},1\leq k\leq n_{i}}Y_{i}^{j},

and
w_{i}^{j}=\frac{1}{m_{i}n_{i}}
, where
j=m_{i}(k-1)+\ell
.
This gives
\mathbf{s}^{-1}(\mathbf{t})
the structure of a weighted branched manifold by patching together
\{\mathbf{s}_{i}^{-1}(\mathbf{t}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
.

Definition 2.2.7
.

A sequence of multisections
\{\mathbf{s}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}

converges to a multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
in the
C^{k}
-topology
(where
k
may be
\infty
), if for each fixed orbibundle chart
(L_{i},\Gamma_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{i})
of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
, which is independent of
n
,
there exist
\ell_{i}
-multisections
\{\mathbf{s}^{\prime n}_{i}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{i}
of
(L_{i},\Gamma_{i})
such that

(1)

\ell_{i}
is independent of
n
,

(2)

\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{i}
is independent of
n
,

(3)

\mathbf{s}^{\prime n}_{i}
is equivalent to
\mathbf{s}^{n}_{i}
,

(4)

\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{i}
is equivalent to
\mathbf{s}_{i}
, and

(5)

\mathbf{s}^{\prime n}_{i}
converges to
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{i}
in the
C^{k}
-topology.

The following is proved in
[
FT
, Prop. 2.4.2]
(also see
[
FO
, Theorem 3.11]
).

Lemma 2.2.8

(Multisection perturbation lemma)
.

Let
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}
be two multisections of an orbibundle
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
, where
\mathbf{s}
is lifted. If the base
\mathsf{X}
is compact, then there exists a sequence
\{\mathbf{s}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of lifted multisections of
\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{X}
such that
\{\mathbf{s}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mathbf{s}
in the
C^{\infty}
-topology and
\mathbf{s}^{n}
is transverse to
\mathbf{t}
.

In practice, we will take
{\bf s}
to be the zero section and
{\bf t}
to be the linearized
\overline{\partial}
-section.

3.
Almost complex structures and moduli spaces

Let
(M^{2n+1},\xi)
be a contact manifold and
\alpha
a nondegenerate contact form for
\xi
. Let
L
be a positive real number. Denote the Reeb vector field of
\alpha
by
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
and the set of possibly multiply-covered closed orbits
\gamma
of
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
with action
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\leq L
by
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
. Also let
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L,\operatorname{good}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
be the subset of good Reeb orbits.

The discussion until the end of Section
6
is valid for curves of any genus.

3.1.
Almost complex structures

We will review some notions from
[
BH
]
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A contact form
\alpha
is called
L
-simple
3
3

3

In
[
BH
]
there is a notion called “
L
-supersimple”, the difference between “
L
-simple” and “
L
-supersimple” is that “
L
-simple” allows the existence of elliptic orbits of action
\leq L
.

if

(1)

each simple orbit
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
of
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
is nondegenerate;

(2)

around
\gamma
there exists an embedding

\Phi_{\gamma}:[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}\to M,

such that
[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]
has coordinate
t
,

\overline{m},\underline{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
satisfy
\overline{m}+\underline{m}=n
,
\delta>0
is small,

\displaystyle D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}=\Big\{(\overline{x},\overline{y})=(\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{\overline{m}},\overline{y}_{1},\dots,\overline{y}_{\overline{m}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\overline{m}}~|~\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\overline{x}_{i}^{2}+\overline{y}_{i}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\Big\},

\displaystyle D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}=\Big\{(\underline{x},\underline{y})=(\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{\underline{m}},\underline{y}_{1},\dots,\underline{y}_{\underline{m}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\underline{m}}~|~\sum_{i=1}^{\underline{m}}\underline{x}_{i}^{2}+\underline{y}_{i}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\Big\},

(0,\overline{x},\overline{y},\underline{x},\underline{y})\sim(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma),\overline{x},\overline{y},-\underline{x},-\underline{y})
,
\gamma=\Phi_{\gamma}([0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times\{0\}\times\{0\})
, and

\Phi_{\gamma}^{*}\alpha=(1+\overline{Q}+\underline{Q})dt+\beta;\quad\mbox{and}

(3)

the images of
\Phi_{\gamma}
are disjoint for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
.

Here
\overline{Q}
is quadratic in
(\overline{x},\overline{y})

(without constant or linear terms),

\underline{Q}
is quadratic in
(\underline{x},\underline{y})
, and

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\frac{1}{2}(\overline{x}_{i}d\overline{y}_{i}-\overline{y}_{i}d\overline{x}_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{\underline{m}}\frac{1}{2}(\underline{x}_{i}d\underline{y}_{i}-\underline{y}_{i}d\underline{x}_{i}).

We denote

(\mathcal{N}_{0},\alpha_{0}):=([0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim},(1+\overline{Q}+\underline{Q})dt+\beta).

Russell Advek pointed out a mistake in the definition of
(\mathcal{N}_{0},\alpha_{0})
in the previous versions and suggested the current version.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Given an
L
-nondegenerate
4
4

4

This means all the orbits with action
\leq L
are nondegenerate
contact form
\alpha^{\prime}
on
M
, there exists a perturbation
\alpha
of
\alpha^{\prime}
on small neighborhoods of simple orbits of
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{L}
such that
\ker\alpha=\ker\alpha^{\prime}
,
\alpha
is
L
-nondegenerate,
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}=\mathcal{P}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{L}
, the orbits have the same
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\alpha
actions, and
\alpha
is
L
-simple.

Proof.

For each simple orbit
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{L}
, let
d\varphi^{t}:\xi_{\gamma(0)}\to\xi_{\gamma(t)}
be the linearized Reeb flow along
\gamma
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)=1.

Writing
p=\gamma(0)
,
d\varphi^{1}:\xi_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\xi_{p}
is a linear symplectic map with symplectic form
\omega^{\prime}_{p}=d\alpha^{\prime}|_{\xi_{p}}
. Since
\gamma
is nondegenerate,
1
is not an eigenvalue of
d\varphi^{1}
.
The contact plane
\xi_{p}
splits as the direct sum
\overline{\xi}_{p}\oplus\underline{\xi}_{p}
of two
d\varphi^{1}
-invariant symplectic subspaces such that
d\varphi^{1}|_{\overline{\xi}_{p}}
has no negative eigenvalue
and
d\varphi^{1}|_{\underline{\xi}_{p}}
has only negative eigenvalues.

Choose an identification
L_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\xi_{p}
,
such that

(1)

L_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2\overline{m}}\oplus 0)=\overline{\xi}_{p}
,

(2)

L_{0}(0\oplus\mathbb{R}^{2\underline{m}})=\underline{\xi}_{p},
and

(3)

L_{0}^{*}\omega^{\prime}_{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}d\overline{x}_{i}\wedge d\overline{y}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{\underline{m}}d\underline{x}_{i}\wedge d\underline{y}_{i}=:\omega.

The symplectic matrix
A=L_{0}^{*}(d\varphi^{1}):\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
splits as
A=\overline{A}\oplus\underline{A}
,
where
\overline{A}\in\operatorname{Sp}(2\overline{m},\mathbb{R})
and
\underline{A}\in\operatorname{Sp}(2\underline{m},\mathbb{R}).

Then there exist matrices
\overline{X}\in\mathfrak{sp}(2\overline{m},\mathbb{R})
and
\underline{X}\in\mathfrak{sp}(2\underline{m},\mathbb{R})
such that
e^{\overline{X}}=\overline{A}
and
e^{\underline{X}}=-\underline{A}
: Indeed, let
\overline{X},\underline{X}
be the principal logarithms of
\overline{A}
and
\underline{A}
(see
[
Hi
, Theorem 1.23]
). By the standard properties of principal logarithms (see
[
Hi
, Theorems 1.13 and 11.2]
),
\overline{X}
,
\underline{X}
lie in the Lie algebras of the corresponding Lie groups.

Consider the vector field
Y=X\cdot(\overline{x},\overline{y},\underline{x},\underline{y})^{T}
on
D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}
with
X=\overline{X}\oplus\underline{X}
.
The Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{Y}\omega=0
, since
\overline{X}\in\mathfrak{sp}(2\overline{m},\mathbb{R})
and
\underline{X}\in\mathfrak{sp}(2\underline{m},\mathbb{R})
.
This implies that
d(Y\lrcorner~\omega)=0
. Let
H:D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Hamiltonian function such that
Y\lrcorner~\omega=dH
. Since
Y
is linear in
\overline{x},\overline{y},\underline{x},\underline{y}
, we may take
H
to be
\overline{Q}+\underline{Q}
, where
\overline{Q}
is quadratic in
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
and
\underline{Q}
is quadratic in
(\underline{x},\underline{y})
. Consider the
1
-form

\alpha_{0}:=(1+H)dt+\beta\quad\mbox{on}\quad[0,1]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}},

where
\beta
is as in Equation (
3.1.1
). It is contact for
\delta>0
sufficiently small and its Reeb vector field is directed by
\partial_{t}+Y
.

For
t\in[0,1]
we extend the definition of
L_{0}
to:

L_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\xi_{\gamma(t)},\quad\zeta\mapsto d\varphi^{t}\circ L_{0}\circ(e^{-t\overline{X}}\oplus e^{-t\underline{X}})\zeta.

Identifying
T_{(t,0,0)}(\{t\}\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}})
with
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
using the basis
\{\partial_{\overline{x}},\partial_{\overline{y}},\partial_{\underline{x}},\partial_{\underline{y}}\}
, we can form

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}:\coprod_{t\in[0,1]}T_{(t,0,0)}(\{t\}\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}})\to\coprod_{t\in[0,1]}T_{\gamma(t)}M,\quad\zeta\mapsto L_{t}\zeta,

Now let
\Phi
be a map from
\mathcal{N}_{0}
to a neighborhood of
\gamma\subset M
such that its tangent map along
[0,1]\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
agrees with
\mathbb{L}
. On
\mathcal{N}_{0}
we have two contact forms
\alpha_{0}
and
\Phi^{*}\alpha^{\prime}
that agree along
[0,1]\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
. By a standard Moser-type argument, there exists a diffeomorphism
\Psi
of
\mathcal{N}_{0}
(after shrinking
\mathcal{N}_{0}
if necessary), such that
\Psi
maps
\ker\alpha_{0}
to
\ker\Phi^{*}\alpha^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\Phi\circ\Psi:\mathcal{N}_{0}\to M
maps
\ker\alpha_{0}
to
\ker\alpha^{\prime}.

Near
\gamma
, we replace the contact form
\alpha^{\prime}
by
(\Phi\circ\Psi)_{*}\alpha_{0}
, and in an annulus region we interpolate between the two.
After the modification, the contact condition, the Reeb orbit
\gamma
, and the linearized return map are preserved, and we do not create additional orbits of action
\leq L
.
∎

Let
Y
be the vector field on
D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}
satisfying

Y\lrcorner~(\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{2\overline{m}}d\overline{x}_{i}\wedge d\overline{y}_{i}+\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{2\underline{m}}d\underline{x}_{i}\wedge d\underline{y}_{i})=dQ,

where
Q=\overline{Q}+\underline{Q}
.
Since
Q
is quadratic,
Y
is also a vector field on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
.
Because
\partial_{t}+Y\in\ker d\alpha
,
there exists a positive function
g
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
such that

(g)

g\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}=\partial_{t}+Y
.

Since
Q
is quadratic in

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=(\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{\overline{m}},\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{\underline{m}},\overline{y}_{1},\dots,\overline{y}_{\overline{m}},\underline{y}_{1},\dots,\underline{y}_{\underline{m}}),

the coefficients of
Y
with respect to
(\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}})
can be written as a column vector
X\cdot(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})^{T}
, where
X
is a
2n\times 2n
matrix with constant real coefficients.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Given a contact form
\alpha
, an almost complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{R}\times M
is
\alpha
-tame
if

(1)

J
is independent of
s
, where
s
is the
\mathbb{R}
-coordinate;

(2)

J(\partial_{s})=g\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
for some positive function
g
on
M
; and

(3)

there exists a
2n
-plane field
\xi^{\prime}
on
M
such that
J\xi^{\prime}=\xi^{\prime}
and
d\alpha(v,Jv)>0
for all nonzero
v\in\xi^{\prime}
.

Given a Reeb orbit
\gamma
of
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
, we write
\gamma^{s}
for the simple orbit underlying
\gamma
and
m(\gamma)
for the multiplicity of
\gamma
over
\gamma^{s}
. Choose a point
x_{\gamma^{s}}
(a
marker
) on each
\gamma^{s}
.

Definition 3.1.4

(
L
-simple for a symplectization)
.

Let
L>0
and
\alpha
be an
L
-simple contact form.

(1) An almost complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{R}\times M
is
L
-simple for
\alpha
if
J
is
\alpha
-tame and inside a sufficiently small neighborhood
\Phi_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}_{0})
(recall
\mathcal{N}_{0}=[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}
) of each simple Reeb orbit
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
we have

(i)

\xi^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\rangle
;

(ii)

J:\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
; and

(iii)

the function
g
satisfying
J(\partial_{s})=g\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
is given by (g) above.

A pair
(\alpha,J)
is said to be
L
-simple pair
, if
\alpha
is
L
-simple and
J
is
L
-simple for
\alpha
.

(2) If
\gamma
is simple, then the above coordinates

(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),

subject to the normalization

(N)

\Phi_{\gamma}(0,0)=x_{\gamma^{s}}
.

are called
simple coordinates for
\gamma
.

(3) If
\gamma
is not simple, then, by abuse of notation, the coordinates

(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

on
[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}
are called
simple coordinates for
\gamma
where

[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}\to[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s})]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}

is the
m(\gamma)
-fold covering map.

Given an
L
-simple
\alpha
, we can construct an
L
-simple
J
as follows: Let
\xi^{\prime}\subset TM
be a
2n
-plane field such that
\xi^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\rangle
on a small neighborhood
V_{\gamma}
of each
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
;
\xi^{\prime}=\xi
outside a slightly larger neighborhood
V^{\prime}_{\gamma}
of each
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{L}
; and
\xi^{\prime}
interpolates between
\mathbb{R}\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{n}}\rangle
and
\xi
on the remaining annular regions. Let
J
be a complex structure of
\xi^{\prime}
such that
d\alpha(v,Jv)>0
for all
0\neq v\in\xi^{\prime}
and
J(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}})=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
on
V_{\gamma}
. We then extend
J
to
\mathbb{R}\times M
by
J(\partial_{s})=g\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
, where
g
is given by (g) on
V_{\gamma}
,
g=1
outside
V^{\prime}_{\gamma}
, and
g
interpolates between the two in the remaining annular regions.

The main reason for using
L
-simple
J
is the following: Let
u
be a
J
-holomorphic curve in
\mathbb{R}\times M
and suppose
u
has an end which converges to
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma
with coordinates
(s,t)
. Then, with respect to the simple coordinates, this end of
u
can be written as
(s,t,\eta(s,t))
, where
\eta
satisfies

(3.1.2)

\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial s}+j_{0}\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial t}+S\eta=0,

j_{0}
is the standard complex structure, and
S:=-j_{0}X
is a
2n\times 2n
symmetric matrix. In other words,
the Cauchy-Riemann equations become linear near the ends for curves that are graphical over
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma
.

Convention 3.1.5
.

We are using the convention that when a matrix (e.g.,
S
) acts on
\eta
or its derivatives from the left,
\eta
or its derivatives are viewed as column vectors.

More generally, let
(W,\alpha)
be a
(2n+2)
-dimensional Liouville cobordism from
(M_{+},\alpha_{+})
to
(M_{-},\alpha_{-})
, i.e.,
W
is compact,
\partial W=M_{+}-M_{-}
,
\alpha
is a
1
-form on
W
such that
d\alpha
is symplectic, and
\alpha_{\pm}=\alpha|_{M_{\pm}}
is a contact form on
M_{\pm}
. Let
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\alpha})
be the completion of
(W,\alpha)
, obtained by smoothly attaching the symplectization ends
[1,\infty)\times M_{+}
and
(-\infty,-1]\times M_{-}
. Let
s
be the
[1,\infty)
- or
(-\infty,-1]
-coordinate at the positive and negative ends of
\widehat{W}
.

Definition 3.1.6

(
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple for a cobordism)
.

A
1
-form
\widehat{\alpha}
on
\widehat{W}
is said to be
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple
if it restricts to
L_{\pm}
-simple contact form
\alpha_{\pm}
at the positive and negative ends.
Given an
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple
\widehat{\alpha}
, an almost complex structure
J
on
\widehat{W}
said to be
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple for
\widehat{\alpha}
,
if it is
d\widehat{\alpha}
-tame and restricts to
J_{\pm}
which is
L_{\pm}
-simple for
\alpha_{\pm}
at the positive and negative ends.
A pair
(\widehat{\alpha},J)
is said to be
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple, if
\widehat{\alpha}
is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple and
J
is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple for
\widehat{\alpha}
.

In this paper we assume that all almost complex structures in symplectizations (resp. cobordisms) are
L
-simple (resp.
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple) for some appropriate
L
(resp.
(L_{+},L_{-})
) and some appropriate contact form
\alpha
(resp.
\widehat{\alpha}
).

Remark 3.1.7
.

Strictly speaking, it is not necessary to use simple
J
in this paper, as most of our discussion carries over to the general case. Simple almost complex structures allow for better control of the ends and also simplify gluing.

3.2.
Riemann surfaces and holomorphic maps

Let
(F,j)
be a closed connected Riemann surface.

Definition 3.2.1
.

A
marked Riemann surface

\mathcal{F}=(F,j,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r})

is a quadruple which consists of
(F,j)
, an
ordered
tuple
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{+}\sqcup\mathbf{p}_{-}
,
\mathbf{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm,1},\dots,p_{\pm,l_{\pm}})
, of distinct points on
F
and an
ordered
tuple
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}_{+}\sqcup\mathbf{r}_{-}
,
\mathbf{r}_{\pm}=(r_{\pm,1},\dots,r_{\pm,l_{\pm}})
, of asymptotic markers at
\mathbf{p}
.
Here an
asymptotic marker

r_{\pm,i}
at a puncture
p_{\pm,i}
is an element of
(T_{p_{\pm,i}}F-\{0\})/\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
We denote
\dot{F}=F-\mathbf{p}
.

Definition 3.2.2

(Equivalence relation
\sim_{0}
)
.

Given two marked Riemann surfaces
\mathcal{F}=(F,j,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r})
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(F^{\prime},j^{\prime},\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
, we write
\mathcal{F}\sim_{0}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
if there is a diffeomorphism
\phi:F\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{\prime}
satisfying
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
(that is,
\phi_{*}j=j^{\prime}
,
\phi(\mathbf{p}_{\pm})=\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{\pm}
, and
\phi_{*}(\mathbf{r}_{\pm})=\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{\pm}
, where the latter two maps are maps of ordered tuples).

Let
(W,\alpha)
be the exact symplectic cobordism from Section
3.1
.

Definition 3.2.3

(Equivalence relation
\sim
)
.

Given marked Riemann surfaces
\mathcal{F}=(F,j,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r})
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(F^{\prime},j^{\prime},\mathbf{p}^{\prime},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
and maps
u:\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
,
u^{\prime}:\dot{F}^{\prime}\to\widehat{W}
, we write
(\mathcal{F},u)\sim(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
if there is a diffeomorphism
\phi:F\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{\prime}
satisfying
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{F},u)=(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
(that is,
\phi_{*}j=j^{\prime}
,
\phi(\mathbf{p}_{\pm})=\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{\pm}
and
\phi_{*}(\mathbf{r}_{\pm})=\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{\pm}
, again as ordered tuples, and
u^{\prime}\circ\phi=u
).

Definition 3.2.4

(Equivalence classes)
.

The equivalence class of
(\mathcal{F},u)
under
\sim
will be denoted by
[\mathcal{F},u]
. If
\widehat{W}=\mathbb{R}\times M
, then the equivalence class of
(\mathcal{F},u)
under
\mathbb{R}
-translations in the target will be denoted by
(\mathcal{F},u)_{\mathbb{R}}
and the equivalence class under
\sim
and
\mathbb{R}
-translations in the target will be denoted by
\llbracket\mathcal{F},u\rrbracket
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}=(\gamma_{+,1},\dots,\gamma_{+,l_{+}})
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}=(\gamma_{-,1},\dots,\gamma_{-,l_{-}})
be ordered tuples of Reeb orbits for
\alpha_{+}
and
\alpha_{-}
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
is the space of equivalence classes
[\mathcal{F},u]
of pairs, where
u
is a
J
-holomorphic map
\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
from
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
(i.e.,
u
is asymptotic to
\gamma_{+,i}
at the positive end near
p_{+,i}
and to
\gamma_{-,i}
at the negative end near
p_{-,i}
) and
u
“maps” the asymptotic markers
r_{\pm,i}
to the markers
x_{\gamma^{s}_{\pm,i}}
. Also the superscript
\operatorname{ind}=k
as in
\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
indicates the subset consisting of
[\mathcal{F},u]
of Fredholm index
\operatorname{ind}(u)=k
.

If
\widehat{W}=\mathbb{R}\times M
, then
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})/\mathbb{R}
is the usual quotient of
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
by
\mathbb{R}
-translations in the target.

Definition 3.2.5
.

The
\alpha
-energy
of a map
u
from
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
is given by

E_{\alpha}(u):=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+})-\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}),

where
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm})=\sum_{i=1}^{l_{\pm}}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i})
.

3.3.
Sorting

We fix an ordering of
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\alpha}
which we denote by
\vartheta
.
In practice, we choose a generic
\alpha
such that the map
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\gamma\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)
, is injective and order the orbits
\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\alpha}
by increasing action.

Definition 3.3.1
.

An ordered tuple of Reeb orbits
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is
sorted
if

\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\underbrace{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{1}}_{i_{1}\text{ copies }},\dots,\underbrace{\gamma_{k},\dots,\gamma_{k}}_{i_{k}\text{ copies }}),

where
\gamma_{i}\neq\gamma_{j}
as long as
i\neq j
. We also allow
k=0
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\varnothing
.
If
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is sorted according to
\vartheta
, i.e., we have
\vartheta(\gamma_{i})<\vartheta(\gamma_{j})
for any
1\leq i<j\leq k
, then
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is said to be
\vartheta
-sorted
.

From now on we assume that all the positive (and likewise all the negative) ends of all the moduli spaces are
\vartheta
-sorted using the action, unless stated otherwise.

3.4.
Puncture reorderings and marker rotations

Let
[(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r}),u]
be an element of
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, or more generally of the weighted Sobolev space
W_{\delta}^{l+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
given in Definition
4.2.1
. As before
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{+}\sqcup\mathbf{p}_{-}
,
\mathbf{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm,1},\dots,p_{\pm,l_{\pm}})
,
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}_{+}\sqcup\mathbf{r}_{-}
, and
\mathbf{r}_{\pm}=(r_{\pm,1},\dots,r_{\pm,l_{\pm}})
.

Definition 3.4.1
.

(1)

A
puncture reordering
takes
[(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r}),u]
to

[(F,j,\sigma_{+}({\bf p}_{+})\sqcup\sigma_{-}({\bf p}_{-}),\sigma_{+}({\bf r}_{+})\sqcup\sigma_{-}({\bf r}_{-})),u],

where
\sigma_{\pm}\in S_{l_{\pm}}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}({\bf p}_{\pm})

\displaystyle=(p_{\pm,\sigma_{\pm}(1)},\dots,p_{\pm,\sigma_{\pm}(l_{\pm})})

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}({\bf r}_{\pm})

\displaystyle=(r_{\pm,\sigma_{\pm}(1)},\dots,r_{\pm,\sigma_{\pm}(l_{\pm})}),

and the reordering preserves
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
(i.e.,
\gamma_{\pm,\sigma_{\pm}(i)}=\gamma_{\pm,i}
).

(2)

A
marker rotation
takes
[(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r}),u]
to
[(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r}^{\prime}),u]
, where we write
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{+}\sqcup\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{-}
and
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{\pm}=(r^{\prime}_{\pm,1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{\pm,l_{\pm}})
and the asymptotic markers
r_{\pm,i}
and
r^{\prime}_{\pm,i}
are mapped to the same marker
x_{\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}}
.

(3)

Let
G(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
be the automorphism group of
W_{\delta}^{l+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
generated by the puncture reorderings and marker rotations.

3.5.
\operatorname{(Symp)}
vs
\operatorname{(Cob)}

Consider the moduli space

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}).

There will always be two cases to consider:

(Symp)

(\widehat{W}=\mathbb{R}\times M,J)
is
\mathbb{R}
-invariant and is viewed as a symplectization. This is the case when defining the differential
d
and proving
d^{2}=0
. We usually take
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
.

\operatorname{(Cob)}

(\widehat{W},J)
is viewed as a cobordism. This is the case when defining chain maps. We allow
(\widehat{W},J)
to be
\mathbb{R}
-invariant (e.g., when
(\widehat{W},J)
is the “identity cobordism”). In the case of a cobordism there is no quotienting by
\mathbb{R}
, even if there is an
\mathbb{R}
-action.

We will write
(\widehat{W},J)\in\operatorname{(Symp)}
or
(\widehat{W},J)\in\operatorname{(Cob)}
to indicate whether
(\widehat{W},J)
is viewed as a symplectization or a cobordism. There are two reasons for this distinction:

•

We do not quotient by the
\mathbb{R}
-action for
\operatorname{(Cob)}
.

•

Trivial cylinders are unperturbed (i.e., are more or less ignored) when considered in (Symp), but are perturbed (i.e., we construct an obstruction bundle
E\to V
where the trivial cylinder is in
V
) when considered in
\operatorname{(Cob)}
.

For the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case, we also require
J
to be

Definition 3.5.1

(
(L_{+},L_{-})
-end-generic)
.

Let
J
be an almost complex structure on
(\widehat{W},\widehat{\alpha})
that is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple for
\widehat{\alpha}
. We say
J
is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-end-generic
,
if for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
with
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{\pm}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm})\leq L_{\pm}
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is no
J
-holomorphic curve
[\mathcal{F},u]\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
that agrees with the trivial half cylinder near some puncture of
\dot{F}
in the region
\widehat{W}_{s\geq T}
or
\widehat{W}_{s\leq-T}
, for some
T>1
.

Remark 3.5.2
.

We require
J
to be
(L_{+},L_{-})
-end-generic, because with it we can add additional marked points to stabilize the domain of a
J
-holomorphic cylinder or plane in a more canonical way.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Given an
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple pair
(\widehat{\alpha},J)
, we can perturb
J
to
J^{\prime}
such that
(\widehat{\alpha},J^{\prime})
is still
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple and
J^{\prime}
is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-end-generic.

Proof.

We restrict to the simpler case where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
consists of only one Reeb orbit
\gamma_{+}
of
\alpha_{+}
with
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{+}}(\gamma_{+})\leq L_{+}
. Technically speaking, this suffices for the current paper; the proof of the general case is quite similar to this special case and is left to the reader.

We perturb
J
inductively, using a triple which we call the
complexity

c(\mathcal{F},u)=(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{+}}(\gamma_{+}),E_{\alpha}(u),-\chi(\dot{F})),

where we are using the lexicographic ordering, to order the moduli spaces of curves. More precisely, we perturb
J
so that all the curves of

\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}):=\mathcal{M}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\gamma_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

have nontrivial positive ends, assuming all curves in
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}^{\prime})
with complexity less than that of
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
have nontrivial positive ends.

Let us write
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
and
\partial\mathcal{M}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}-\mathcal{M}
.

Step 1.

We claim that there exists an open neighborhood
V\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}
of
\partial\mathcal{M}
such that no holomorphic curve in
V-\partial\mathcal{M}
has trivial positive end. During the proof of the lemma we call a holomorphic building (See Section 7 of
[
BEHWZ
]
for the definition of holomorphic buildings) whose topmost level is a branched cover with at least one branch point over a trivial cylinder a
b
-building
.

Consider a holomorphic building in
\partial\mathcal{M}
of the form
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
, where
u_{0}
maps to
\widehat{W}
and
u_{1}
maps to
\mathbb{R}\times M_{+}
. (The other types of holomorphic buildings in
\partial\mathcal{M}
can be treated similarly and are left to the reader.) We choose a small neighborhood
U\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}
of
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
such that no holomorphic curve
u\in U-\partial\mathcal{M}
has a trivial positive end as follows: If
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
is not a
b
-building, then
u_{1}
does not have a trivial positive end by unique continuation (here we are assuming that
u_{1}
is not a trivial cylinder) and there is a sufficiently small neighborhood
U
of
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
with the desired property. Next suppose that
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
is a
b
-building and there exists a sequence
\{u^{k}\}
of curves in
\mathcal{M}
that limit to
u_{0}\cup u_{1}
and have trivial positive ends. Then there exist constants
s_{0}
and
k_{0}
such that:

•

J|_{[s_{0},\infty)\times M_{+}}=J_{+}
; and

•

for
k\geq k_{0}
,
u^{k}
restricted to
[s_{0},\infty)\times M_{+}
is a branched cover of
[s_{0},\infty)\times\gamma^{s}_{+}
.

If we replace the portion of
u^{k}
in
[s_{0},\infty)\times M_{+}
by a trivial half-cylinder without branch points, we obtain a holomorphic curve with a trivial positive end in
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}^{\prime})
of lower complexity, which contradicts the inductive assumption. Since
\partial\mathcal{M}
is compact, we can cover
\partial\mathcal{M}
by finitely many open sets
U
and the claim follows.

Step 2.

Recall from Section
3.1
that, for any
[\mathcal{F},u]\in\mathcal{M}
, on a neighborhood of the puncture
p_{+}
of
\dot{F}
where
u
converges to
\gamma_{+}
, we have
u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t))
, where
\eta
satisfies Equation (
3.1.2
).

We denote by
{\mathfrak{C}}
the space of functions
f=(\overline{f},\underline{f}):[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\to\mathbb{R}^{2\overline{m}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\underline{m}}
that satisfies
\overline{f}(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=\overline{f}(0)
and
\underline{f}(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=-\underline{f}(0).

Define a self-adjoint operator, called the
normal asymptotic operator
,
5
5

5

This is slightly different from the full asymptotic operator which appears in Section
4.4
.

A:=-j_{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-S:W^{1,2}(\mathfrak{C})\to L^{2}(\mathfrak{C}),

where
S=-j_{0}X
.
Then
\eta
satisfies

(3.5.1)

\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial s}=A\eta.

Let

\dots\leq\theta_{-2}\leq\theta_{-1}<0<\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{2}\leq\dots

be the eigenvalues of
A
and

\dots,g_{-2},g_{-1},g_{1},g_{2},\dots

be the associated complete set of orthonormal eigenfunctions.
We can write

\eta(s,t)=\sum_{i\leq-1}c_{i}e^{\theta_{i}s}g_{i}(t),

where the
c_{i}:=c_{i}^{\gamma_{+}}(u)
are constants. Observe that
u
has trivial positive end if and only if all the
c_{i}
are zero.

Step 3.

Choose a compact subset
K
of
\mathcal{M}
such that
K\cup V=\overline{\mathcal{M}}
. Below we explain how to make a perturbation of
J
such that all the curves in
K
have no trivial positive end and yet preserve this property for curves near
V
and in lower inductive strata.

Case I
(
\gamma_{+}
is simple). Since
K
is compact, there exists
s_{1}>0
such that:

(1)

J|_{[s_{1},\infty)\times M_{+}}=J_{+}
; and

(2)

for any
[\mathcal{F},u]\in K
the restriction of
u
to
(s_{1},\infty)\times M_{+}
has image in
(s_{1},\infty)\times\gamma_{+}\times D_{\delta^{\prime}}
and can be expressed as
(s,t,\eta(s,t))
, where
\eta
satisfies Equation (
3.5.1
).

Here
0<\delta^{\prime}<\frac{\delta}{2}
, where
\delta
is the constant that appears in Definition
3.1.1
.

Next choose
s_{1}<R
and
\varrho_{1},\dots,\varrho_{k}>0
sufficiently small; we assume that
R
is much larger than the
R
used when perturbing the
J
for the lower inductive strata. Let
\mu:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth bump function that is supported on
(0,3)
and satisfies
\mu([1,2])=1
, and let
\mu_{T}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be its translate
\mu_{T}(s)=\mu(s-T)
.

We perturb
J
to
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime}_{\varrho_{1},\dots,\varrho_{k}}
on the region
[R,R+3]\times\gamma_{+}\times D_{\delta/2}\subset\widehat{W}
by setting

J^{\prime}(\partial_{s})=g\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}-\sum_{j=1}^{k}j_{0}\varrho_{j}\mu_{R}(s)g_{-j}(t),\quad J^{\prime}(\partial_{x_{i}})=\partial_{y_{i}}

on
[R,R+3]\times\gamma_{+}\times D_{\delta^{\prime}}
. The perturbation
\eta^{\prime}
of
\eta
satisfies the equation

(3.5.2)

\frac{\partial\eta^{\prime}}{\partial s}=A\eta^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\varrho_{j}\mu_{R}(s)g_{-j}(t).

All but finitely many Fourier coefficients of
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are the same, i.e., those of
g_{-j}(t)
,
j=1,\dots,k
, which we denote by
f_{-j}(s)g_{-j}(t)
. The coefficient
f_{-j}(s)
satisfies

\frac{df_{-j}}{ds}=\theta_{-j}f_{-j}(s)+\varrho_{j}\mu_{R}(s),

subject to the condition
f_{-j}(s)=c_{-j}e^{\theta_{-j}s}
for
s<R
. We compute

f_{-j}(s)=e^{\theta_{-j}s}\left(c_{-j}+\int_{0}^{s}e^{-\theta_{-j}\sigma}\varrho_{j}\mu_{R}(\sigma)d\sigma\right).

This shows that the Fourier coefficients of all the curves in
K
are perturbed in the
g_{-j}(t)
component by a fixed small constant that depends on
\varrho_{j}
.

We now assume without loss of generality that
J
regular. Since
\gamma_{+}
is simple,
K
is part of a transversely cut out moduli space and the map

K\to\mathbb{R}^{k},\quad[\mathcal{F},u]\mapsto(c_{-1}^{\gamma_{+}}(u),\dots,c_{-k}^{\gamma_{+}}(u))

is smooth. Hence, by Sard’s theorem, for
k>0
sufficiently large, there exist
\varrho_{1},\dots,\varrho_{k}
arbitrarily small such that no holomorphic curve of
J^{\prime}_{\varrho_{1},\dots,\varrho_{k}}
that is close to a curve in
K
has trivial positive end.

Case II
(
\gamma_{+}
is not simple). If
\gamma_{+}
is a
k
-fold cover of some simple
\gamma^{s}_{+}
, then the
k
-fold multiple cover of the
i
th eigenfunction associated to
\gamma^{s}_{+}
is more or less the
ki
th eigenfunction associated to
\gamma_{+}
. The only modification we need to make is to perturb
J
using the eigenfunctions
g_{-j}(t)
associated to
\gamma^{s}_{+}
.

Finally we discuss the effect of perturbing
J
on the “end-genericity” of lower inductive strata and curves close to
V
:

•

The perturbation of
J
does not affect
J_{+}
and the ends of curves in the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times M_{+}
.

•

Since
\varrho_{1},\dots,\rho_{k}
are arbitrarily small, for moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}_{-})
of lower complexity, the curves that are not close to
b
-buildings still do not have trivial positive ends.

•

The curves of
\mathcal{M}(\gamma_{+}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}_{-})
that are close to
b
-buildings also do not have trivial positive ends since the considerations of Step 1 reduce the issue to the “end-genericity” of moduli spaces of even lower complexity.

•

Similarly, the curves that are close to
V
still do not have trivial positive ends.

This completes the proof of Lemma
3.5.3
. ∎

In this paper, we always assume
J
is
(L_{+},L_{-})
-end-generic for some appropriate
(L_{+},L_{-})
when
(\widehat{W},J)\in(\operatorname{Cob})
.

3.6.
Teichmüller space and mapping class group

In this subsection let us fix
(F,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r})
and assume that
\dot{F}=F-{\bf p}
has negative Euler characteristic. For each
p\in\mathbf{p}
fix an identification
N_{p}-\{p\}\simeq(0,\infty)\times S^{1}
, where
N_{p}\subset F
is a small neighborhood of
p
.

Let
\operatorname{Diff}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
be the group of diffeomorphisms of
F
that fix
{\bf p}
and
{\bf r}
as ordered sets and let
\operatorname{Diff}^{\prime}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
be the subgroup generated by the identity component together with Dehn twists
\tau_{p}
along loops around all the
p\in{\bf p}
.

We make the following
slightly nonstandard definition:

Definition 3.6.1
.

\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
is the Teichmüller space of equivalence classes
[j]
of complex

structures on
F
, where two complex structures
j
and
j^{\prime}
are equivalent if and only if there exists
g\in\operatorname{Diff}^{\prime}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
such that
g_{*}j=j^{\prime}
.

Observe that
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
is diffeomorphic the product of the usual Teichmüller space
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p})
with
\times_{p\in{\bf p}}((T_{p}F-\{0\})/\mathbb{R}^{+})
(the set of tuples of markers indexed by
{\bf p}
); this is how we will view
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
from now on.

Given an open set
\mathcal{U}
of
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
, we write
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{[j]}
to indicate that
[j]\in\mathcal{U}
. Also let
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
be a
Teichmüller slice
over
\mathcal{U}
, i.e., a smooth choice of complex structure
j_{x}
on
F
in each equivalence class
x\in\mathcal{U}
subject to the condition that:

(
\#
)

for each
p\in\mathbf{p}
,
j_{x}
agrees with the standard complex structure
j
on some
[R(x),\infty)\times S^{1}
, where
R(x)>0
depends on
x
.

The mapping class group
6
6

6

Again, slightly nonstandardly defined.

\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r}):=\operatorname{Diff}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})/\operatorname{Diff}^{\prime}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})

acts properly discontinuously on
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
.

We denote the stabilizer of
[j]
by

(3.6.1)

\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})_{[j]}=\{[g]\in\text{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})~|~[g]([j])=[j]\}.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

The stabilizer
\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})_{[j]}
is trivial.

Proof.

\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})_{[j]}
is isomorphic to the automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r})
of

(F,j)
which takes
{\bf p}
to itself and
{\bf r}
to itself as ordered sets. The automorphism group of
(F,j)
taking
{\bf p}
to itself is finite since
\chi(\dot{F})<0
and there are no nontrivial automorphisms of finite order that preserve
{\bf r}
.
∎

4.
Fredholm theory

In this section we fix
(F,{\bf p})
and hence
\dot{F}
.

4.1.
Canonical cylindrical coordinates

Let
(\mathcal{F},u)
be a pair where
u:\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
is a sufficiently differentiable map which is asymptotic to
\gamma_{+,i}
at the positive end near
p_{+,i}
and to
\gamma_{-,i}
at the negative end near
p_{-,i}
. Since
J
is
L
-simple, there exist simple coordinates
(t,x,y)
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}\times D
about every orbit
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{L}_{\alpha}
. If
u
can be written as a graph
(s,t)\mapsto(s,t,\eta_{\pm,i}(s,t))
on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i}))\times D
with respect to simple coordinates near the end corresponding to the puncture
p_{\pm,i}\in\mathbf{p}
, then
(s,t)
is said to be the
canonical cylindrical coordinates on
\dot{F}
near
p_{\pm,i}
with respect to
u
.

By the simplicity of
J
, if
[\mathcal{F},u]\in\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, then the canonical cylindrical coordinates for a representative
(\mathcal{F},u)
near the punctures are holomorphic coordinates on
\dot{F}
. On the other hand,
(s,t)
is not necessarily holomorphic if
u
is not
J
-holomorphic.

If
\llbracket\mathcal{F},u\rrbracket\in\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})/\mathbb{R}
, the coordinates
(s,t)
for
(\mathcal{F},u)
are canonical up to translations in the
s
-direction.

4.2.
Fredholm setup

Fix an
L
-simple
J
on the symplectization
\widehat{W}=\mathbb{R}\times M
or an
(L_{+},L_{-})
-simple
J
on the cobordism
\widehat{W}
in the class
C^{\infty}
.

Recall the coordinate
s
at the ends of
\widehat{W}
. Fix a Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
\widehat{W}
which is
s
-invariant at the ends. For simplicity we assume that
g_{0}
restricts to the standard flat metric

g_{0}=ds^{2}+dt^{2}+\sum_{i}dx_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i}dy_{i}^{2}

on the
L
-simple coordinate charts (and hence
g_{0}=g_{0}^{L}
, i.e., depends on
L
). Let
\exp:T\widehat{W}\to\widehat{W}
be the exponential map with respect to
g_{0}
,
\epsilon>0
be a constant smaller than the injectivity radius of
g_{0}
, and

D_{\epsilon}\subset T\widehat{W}=\{(w,\xi)~|~w\in W,|\xi|_{g_{0}}<\epsilon\}

be the
\epsilon
-disk bundle of
T\widehat{W}
.

For the rest of the paper we assume that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
satisfies
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+})<L
, unless indicated otherwise.

4.2.1.
Weighted Sobolev spaces

As in Section
3.6
, we fix an identification

N_{p}-\{p\}\simeq(0,\infty)\times S^{1}

for each
p\in\mathbf{p}
, where
N_{p}\subset F
is a small neighborhood of
p
, and assume that all
j
on
F
or
\dot{F}
satisfy
(\#)
.
7
7

7

This identification is different from the canonical cylindrical coordinates from Section
4.1
.
We also choose a Riemannian metric on
\dot{F}
that restricts to a product metric on all the cylindrical ends
(0,\infty)\times S^{1}
.

Let
\delta>0
be a small positive number. We define the “weighted Sobolev space”
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, which is similar to but slighlty different from Dragnev’s
[
Dr
, Definition 2.6]
:

Definition 4.2.1

(Weighted Sobolev space of maps
\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
)
.

Choose an auxiliary complex structure
j
on
F
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the space of smooth maps
u:\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
that agree with
(j,J)
-holomorphic maps parametrizing trivial holomorphic half-cylinders
[R,+\infty)\times\gamma_{+,i}
near
p_{+,i}
and
(-\infty,-R]\times\gamma_{-,i}
near
p_{-,i}
for some
R
that depends on
u
. Then the
weighted Sobolev space of maps
\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
is

W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}):=\{\exp(u,\xi)~|~u\in\mathcal{C},\xi\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})\},

where
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})
is the usual weighted Sobolev space of sections of
u^{*}D_{\epsilon}
, defined using
g_{0}
, and we are using a smooth weight function
f_{\delta}:\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
which agrees with
e^{\delta|s|}
at the ends of
\widehat{W}
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

The definition of
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
is independent of the choice of
j
: Let
\phi:D^{2}\to D^{2}
, where
D^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
, be a holomorphic map such that
\phi(0)=0
and
\phi^{\prime}(0)\not=0
. Identifying
D^{2}-\{0\}
with the holomorphic half-cylinder
[0,\infty)\times S^{1}
, the independence is a consequence of the fact that
\phi:[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\to[0,\infty)\times S^{1}
exponentially approaches
(s,t)\mapsto(s+a,t+b)
for some
a,b
as
s\to\infty
.

Given
u\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, let
\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}
be the vector space spanned by sections
u^{*}\tilde{\partial}_{s,\pm,i},u^{*}\tilde{\partial}_{t,\pm,i}
of
C^{\infty}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})
, where
i=1,\dots,l_{\pm}
;
R\gg 0
;
f:[R,\infty)\to[0,1]
is a smooth map satisfying

f(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{for $s\geq 2R$},\\
0&\mbox{for $s\leq 2R-1$};\end{array}\right.

\tilde{\partial}_{s,+,i}=f(s)\partial_{s}
and
\tilde{\partial}_{t,+,i}=f(s)\partial_{t}
on
[R,\infty)
times an
L
-simple coordinate chart for
\gamma_{+,i}
; and
\tilde{\partial}_{s,-,i},\tilde{\partial}_{t,-,i}
are defined analogously using
f(-s)
. The topology on
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
comes from the topology on
\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}\times W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})
via the exponential map
\exp(u,\mathfrak{a}+\xi)
, where
(\mathfrak{a},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}\times W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})
.

Also note that the Sobolev norm depends on
g_{0}
and
f_{\delta}
, but the topology on this Sobolev space does not depend on the particular choices of
g_{0}
and
f_{\delta}
.

Remark 4.2.3

(Remark on values of
k
and
p
)
.

By the Sobolev embedding theorem, if
k>r+\frac{m}{p}
, then
W^{k,p}(\Omega)\subset C^{r}(\Omega)
for any compact domain
\Omega
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with smooth boundary; see Remark
4.2.4
for obtaining the Sobolev embedding theorem for
\dot{F}
. In our case
m=\dim\dot{F}=2
and we take
r\geq 2
, since we want at least two continuous derivatives (we need one continuous derivative for defining
s_{\pm,i}
in Step 1B in the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
and two continuous derivatives to ensure that Lemma
7.6.7
holds). Hence from now on we assume that
k>r+1
,
p=2
, and
r\geq 2
.

The following is well-known:

Remark 4.2.4
.

We can use the Sobolev embedding theorem for compact domains to show that there exists a global constant
C>0
such that for all

\xi\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}D_{\epsilon})

we have

(4.2.1)

|f_{\delta}\xi|_{C^{r}}\leq C\|f_{\delta}\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}}=C\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}}.

Suppose for simplicity that
\dot{F}
has only one end and
\dot{F}
is written as the union
\Omega_{0}\cup\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}\dots
of compact domains, where each
\Omega_{i}\cap\Omega_{i+1}
is a circle,
\Omega_{0}
is the “thick part”, and
\Omega_{i}
,
i>0
, is biholomorphic to the standard
S^{1}\times[0,1]
. Then we apply the Sobolev embedding theorem for each
\Omega_{i}
, noting that

\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\Omega_{i})}\leq\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F})}

and that the Sobolev spaces
W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\Omega_{i})
and
W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\Omega_{i+1})
can be identified for
i\gg 0
because
u^{*}D_{\epsilon}
is asymptotically cylindrical at the ends of
\dot{F}
. Hence the same constant
C>0
can be used when we apply the Sobolev embedding theorem to all
\Omega_{i}
.

Let
W_{\delta}^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})
be the usual weighted Sobolev space of sections of

\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W}=\wedge^{0,1}\dot{F}\otimes_{J}u^{*}T\widehat{W}\to\dot{F}

in the class
(k,p)
with weight
f_{\delta}
.

Remark 4.2.5
.

In the functional analysis setup we use
W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}
and
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
spaces for large
k
to extract a collection of finite-dimensional bundles
E\to V
. Once this is done we throw away the Sobolev space setup and work in the category of finite-dimensional manifolds or orbifolds. (This is analogous to the
[
FO
]
and
[
FO3
]
setup and differs from that of
[
HWZ3
]
.)

4.2.2.
\chi(\dot{F})<0

We first treat the case where
\dot{F}
has negative Euler characteristic.
Given a Teichmüller slice
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
of an open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r}),u)\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}j\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},u\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}),\\
u(r_{\pm,i})=x_{\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}}\end{array}\right.\right\},

where the pair
(F,\mathbf{p})
is fixed (as stipulated at the beginning of this section).
We define the bundle

(4.2.2)

\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}:\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})\to\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}},

whose fiber over
(\mathcal{F},u)
is

(\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}})_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=W_{\delta}^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W}).

Then
\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
is a section of
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
defined by

\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}(\mathcal{F},u)=(\mathcal{F},u,\tfrac{1}{2}(du+J(u)du\circ j)).

Also let

(4.2.3)

\Pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}:\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\quad(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r}),u)\mapsto j

be the projection to
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
.

When the Teichmüller slice
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
is understood, it will often be omitted from the notation, e.g.,
\overline{\partial}_{J}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{E}
.

Let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection on
T\widehat{W}
with respect to the metric
g_{0}
. Let
L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
be the differential

(\overline{\partial}_{J})_{*}:T_{(\mathcal{F},u)}\mathcal{B}\to T_{(\mathcal{F},u,\overline{\partial}_{J}u)}\mathcal{E}

postcomposed with the projection to
W_{\delta}^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W}).
Then
L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
can be written as:

(4.2.4)

L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}:T\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(j)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}\oplus W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})\to W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W}),

(\mathfrak{j},\mathfrak{a},\xi)\mapsto Y(\mathfrak{j})+D_{u}(\mathfrak{a}+\xi),

where
Y(\mathfrak{j})
is induced from the variation of
j
in the direction on
\mathfrak{j}
,
D_{u}
is the usual linearized
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-operator with fixed
j
(which depends on the choice of
\nabla
if
u
is not
J
-holomorphic), and
\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}
is as given in Section
4.2.1
. Here
l_{+}+l_{-}
is the total number of punctures of
\dot{F}
.

4.2.3.
Topology for
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

The topology for
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
is generated by the topologies
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
from the right-hand side of

(4.2.5)

\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})\cap(\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}/\sim)=(\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}})^{-1}(0),

where we are ranging over all open sets
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{[j]}
; the topology on
