(\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}})^{-1}(0)
is induced from
\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
; and we note that
\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})_{[j]}=1
by Lemma
3.6.2
.

We claim that
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
does not depend on the choice of Teichmüller slice: Given Teichmüller slices
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{U}
, there is a smooth family of diffeomorphisms
\phi_{x}:F\xrightarrow{\sim}F
isotopic to the identity parametrized by
x\in\mathcal{U}
such that
(\phi_{x})_{*}j_{x}=j^{\prime}_{x}
, where
j_{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
j^{\prime}_{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
are lifts of
x\in\mathcal{U}
. Let
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathcal{U}
be close with respect to some metric (e.g., the Teichmüller metric)
d
on
\mathcal{U}
and
u_{0},u_{1}\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
be
(j^{\prime}_{x_{i}},J)
-holomorphic maps that are close. By Section
4.2.1
,
u_{1}=\exp(u_{0},\mathfrak{a}+\xi)
for
(\mathfrak{a},\xi)
with small norm
\|\cdot\|
in
\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}\times W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},u_{0}^{*}D_{\epsilon})
.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Writing
u_{1}\circ\phi_{x_{1}}=\exp(u_{0}\circ\phi_{x_{0}},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime})
, where

(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2(l_{+}+l_{-})}\times W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},(u_{0}\circ\phi_{x_{0}})^{*}D_{\epsilon}),

and
\phi_{x_{1}}=\exp(\phi_{x_{0}},\eta)
, we have:

(4.2.6)

\|\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime}\|<C(d(x_{1},x_{0})+\|(\mathfrak{a},\xi)\|)

for some constant
C>0
which depends on
C^{m}
-norms of derivatives of
\phi_{x_{0}}
,
\phi_{x_{0}}^{-1}
and
C^{m}
-norms of
\mathfrak{a}+\xi
and
\eta
.

Lemma
4.2.6
then implies the claim.

Proof of Lemma
4.2.6
.

The proof uses Remark
4.2.2
. We first do some model calculations.

Model calculations.

(1) Given
f\in W^{1,3}(\mathbb{R})
, we want to compare
\|f\|_{W^{1,3}}=\|f\|+\|f^{\prime}\|+\|f^{\prime\prime}\|
, where
\|f\|
is the
L^{2}
-norm, with
\|f\circ\phi\|_{W^{1,3}}
, where

(*)

\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a diffeomorphism such that
\phi
limits to
x\mapsto x+c_{\pm}
and
\phi^{\prime}
to
1
at the positive and negative ends.

(Note that the maps
\phi_{x}
have this form.) By the change of variables formula and our requirement on
\phi^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\|f\circ\phi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int|f\circ\phi(x)|^{2}dx\leq|\tfrac{1}{\phi^{\prime}}|_{C^{0}}\int|f\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|dx

\displaystyle=|\tfrac{1}{\phi^{\prime}}|_{C^{0}}\int|f(y)|^{2}dy\leq C^{2}\|f\|^{2}

for some constant
C>0
which depends on
|(\phi^{-1})^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\|(f\circ\phi)^{\prime}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int|(f\circ\phi)^{\prime}(x)|^{2}dx=\int|f^{\prime}\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq|\phi^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}\int|f^{\prime}\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|dx

\displaystyle=|\phi^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}\int|f^{\prime}(y)|^{2}dy\leq C^{2}\|f^{\prime}\|^{2},

for some constant
C>0
which depends on
|\phi^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}
and

\displaystyle\|(f\circ\phi)^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}=\int|(f\circ\phi)^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int(|f^{\prime\prime}(\phi(x))|^{2}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|^{4}+2|f^{\prime\prime}(\phi(x))f^{\prime}(\phi(x))||\phi^{\prime}(x)|^{2}|\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)|+|f^{\prime}(\phi(x))|^{2}|\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2})dx

\displaystyle\leq|\phi^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}^{3}\int|f^{\prime\prime}(y)|^{2}dy+2|\phi^{\prime\prime}|_{C^{0}}|\phi^{\prime}|_{C^{0}}\int|f^{\prime\prime}(y)f^{\prime}(y)|dy+|\phi^{\prime\prime}|_{C^{0}}|\tfrac{1}{\phi^{\prime}}|_{C^{0}}\int|f^{\prime}(y)|^{2}dy

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}+\|f^{\prime}\|^{2}),

where
C>0
depends on
C^{m}
-norms of derivatives of
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
. Hence

\|f\circ\phi\|_{W^{1,3}}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,3}}.

(2) We also estimate
\|(f^{2}\circ\phi)^{\prime}\|^{2}
:

\displaystyle(f^{2}\circ\phi)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(2ff^{\prime})(\phi(x))\phi^{\prime}(x),

\displaystyle(f^{2}\circ\phi)^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(2ff^{\prime})(\phi(x))\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)+(2ff^{\prime\prime}+2(f^{\prime})^{2})(\phi(x))\phi^{\prime}(x),

\displaystyle\|(f^{2}\circ\phi)^{\prime}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C|f|_{C^{1}}^{2}(\|f^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}+\|f^{\prime}\|^{2}),

where
C>0
depends on
C^{m}
-norms of derivatives of
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
.

(3) Next consider
W^{1,3}_{g_{\delta}}(\mathbb{R})
, where
g_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a smooth weight function that has the form
e^{\delta|x|}
outside of
[-1,1]
with
\delta>0
. We treat the first derivative:

\displaystyle\|f\circ\phi\|^{2}_{g_{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\int|f\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|g_{\delta}(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq|\tfrac{1}{\phi^{\prime}}|_{C^{0}}|\tfrac{g_{\delta}}{g_{\delta}\circ\phi}|^{2}_{C^{0}}\int|f\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|g_{\delta}\circ\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi^{\prime}(x)|dx\leq C\|f\|^{2}_{g_{\delta}},

in view of the fact that
\tfrac{g_{\delta}}{g_{\delta}\circ\phi}
is bounded (at the positive end limits to
\tfrac{e^{\delta x}}{e^{\delta(x+c_{+})}}=e^{-\delta c_{+}}
). The estimates easily generalize to
W^{k,p}_{g_{\delta}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Verification of Estimate (
4.2.6
).
Since
u_{1}=\exp(u_{0},\mathfrak{a}+\xi)
, we have

\displaystyle u_{1}\circ\phi_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle=\exp(u_{0}\circ\phi_{x_{1}},\mathfrak{a}\circ\phi_{x_{1}}+\xi\circ\phi_{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=\exp(u_{0}\circ\phi_{x_{0}},\mathfrak{a}\circ\phi_{x_{0}}+\xi\circ\phi_{x_{0}}+du_{0}(\phi_{x_{0}})\eta+Q_{u_{0}}),

where
Q_{u_{0}}
is quadratic in
\mathfrak{a},\xi,\eta
and depends on
u_{0}
.
The same argument as in (1) and (3) of the model calculation — the key point is that, by Remark
4.2.2
, at the ends of
\dot{F}
with coordinates
(s,t)
,
\phi_{x_{0}}
and
\phi_{x_{1}}
asymptotically have the form
(s,t)\mapsto(s+c,t+d)
, where
c,d
are constant — implies that:

\displaystyle\|\xi\circ\phi_{x_{0}}\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\xi\|,

where
C
depends on the
C^{m}
-norms of derivatives of the derivatives of
\phi_{x_{0}}
and
\phi^{-1}_{x_{0}}
. We also have:

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{a}\circ\phi_{x_{0}}\|\leq C\|\mathfrak{a}\|,

\displaystyle\|du_{0}(\phi_{x_{0}})\eta\|\leq Cd(x_{1},x_{0}),

\displaystyle\|Q_{u_{0}}\|\leq C(d(x_{1},x_{0})+\|(\mathfrak{a},\xi)\|),

where
C
in the last estimate also depends on the
C^{m}
-norms of
\mathfrak{a}+\xi
and
\eta
. Note that the
C^{m}
-norm of
\mathfrak{a}+\xi
is controlled by the
C^{0}
-norm by elliptic regularity and the exponential decay of the ends.
Putting the inequalities together yields Estimate (
4.2.6
).
∎

4.2.4.
The map
I_{\gamma}

Let
(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})
be a representative of

[\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0}]\in\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

and let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
be a small neighborhood of
(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})
inside

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})=

\displaystyle\left\{(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{r}),u)\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}u\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}),\\
u(r_{\pm,i})=x_{\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}}\end{array}\right.\right\}

Consider the positive end of
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
asymptotic to
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
. Then on this end
u
is graphical, i.e., can be written in canonical cylindrical coordinates on
\dot{F}
and simple coordinates on the target as

u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t)),

after lifting to the
m(\gamma)
-fold cover
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z})\times D
. We assume that the ray
\{s\in[R,\infty),t=0\}\subset\dot{F}
corresponds to the asymptotic marker
r_{+}
which “maps” to
\gamma
.

We then define a map

\displaystyle I=I_{\gamma}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}\times[T^{\prime},+\infty)\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}((t,\eta(s,t))|_{s=s^{\prime}}),

where
T^{\prime}
is a constant such that we can write
u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t))
for any
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
and
s\geq T^{\prime}
.
Clearly
I((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})
is independent of the choice of sufficiently close
(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})
.

Lemma 4.2.7
.

I
is a
C^{1}
-map.

Lemma
4.2.7
will be proved in the Appendix.

4.2.5.
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0

We now consider the case where
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
, namely when
\chi(\dot{F})=0
or
1
.

Lemma 4.2.8
.

In the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case, suppose
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}\not=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
. Given a compact subset
K\subset\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})/\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small such that for each representative of
(\mathcal{F},u)
of each
[\mathcal{F},u]\in K
and
\gamma\subset\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
there exists a value

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u):=\max\{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}~|~I_{\gamma}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)-\varepsilon^{\prime}\}.

Moreover, if
u_{T}
is
u
translated up by
s=T
units, then

s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u_{T})=s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u)+T.

The same holds for
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
which is
C^{1}
-close to some representative
(\mathcal{F},u)
of
K
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from observing that
(\mathcal{F},u)
cannot be a branched cover of a trivial cylinder by Euler characteristic reasons (note that
u
also cannot be a trivial cylinder since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}\not=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
) and that
I_{\gamma}((\mathcal{F},u),s)
is a strictly increasing function of
s
if
u
is not a branched cover of a trivial cylinder. The second and third assertions are immediate.
∎

Lemma 4.2.9
.

In the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case, there exists a small compactly supported perturbation
J^{\prime}
of
J
such that, given a compact subset
K\subset\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, there exists
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
small such that for each representative of
(\mathcal{F},u)
of each
[\mathcal{F},u]\in\mathcal{M}_{J^{\prime}}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
and
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
there exists a value

s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u):=\max\{{s^{\prime}\in[1,+\infty)}~|~I_{\gamma}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)-\varepsilon^{\prime}\}.

The same holds for
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
which is
C^{1}
-close to some representative
(\mathcal{F},u)
of
K
.

Proof.

We only need to ensure that there are no holomorphic cylinders of
(\widehat{W},J^{\prime})
that agree with trivial half-cylinders at the positive end of
\widehat{W}
. This is done by taking an end-generic perturbation
J^{\prime}
of
J
given in Lemma
3.5.3
.
∎

We analogously define
s^{\prime}_{\gamma_{,}-}(\mathcal{F},u)
for
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
by replacing
\max
by
\min
and
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)-\varepsilon^{\prime}
by
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)+\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

From now on we assume that in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case
J
is sufficiently generic so that the conclusion of Lemma
4.2.9
holds. We write
s^{\prime}_{+}
and
s^{\prime}_{-}
instead of
s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}
and
s^{\prime}_{\gamma,-}
if
\gamma
is understood.

We now define the unordered set
{\bf q}:=\cup_{\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}\cup\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}}{\bf q}_{\gamma}
of punctures for
(\mathcal{F},u)
as follows, provided
s^{\prime}_{\gamma,\pm}(\mathcal{F},u)
exist:

(Q)

Identify
u^{-1}(\{s={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}_{\pm}(\mathcal{F},u)}\})\simeq\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}
by projecting to the
t
-coordinate, where we are still using simple coordinates for
\gamma
. Pick the point
t=0\in\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to the asymptotic marker and then define

{\bf q}_{\gamma}=\{(s,t)=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}_{\pm}(\mathcal{F},u)},\tfrac{k}{2m(\gamma)}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))~|~k=1,\dots,2m(\gamma)\}.

Let us write
\ddot{F}=\dot{F}-{\bf q}
; since the cardinality of
\bf q_{\gamma}
is
2m(\gamma)
, we have
\chi(\ddot{F})<0
. (Later on, when we specifically treat contact homology, there is only one
\gamma
.)

Change of Notation 4.2.10
.

From now on, we write

(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u),

with the understanding that
{\bf q}=\varnothing
when
\chi(\dot{F})<0
,
{\bf q}
is an unordered set,
{\bf p}
and
{\bf q}
are disjoint, and
{\bf q}
are removable punctures. We modify Definitions
3.2.2
and
3.2.3
so that the equivalence relations

\mathcal{F}=(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})\sim_{0}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(F,j^{\prime},{\bf p},{\bf q}^{\prime},{\bf r}^{\prime})

and
(\mathcal{F},u)\sim(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
require
\phi({\bf q})={\bf q}^{\prime}
as unordered sets in addition. Also we modify the definitions of
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
,
\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
, and
\operatorname{Aut}(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf r})
to
\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})
,
\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})
, and
\operatorname{Aut}(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})
so that diffeomorphisms of
F
take
{\bf q}
to
{\bf q}
setwise in addition.

Puncture reorderings and marker rotations are assumed to take
{\bf q}
to
{\bf q}
setwise and the automorphism group of
W_{\delta}^{l+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
generated by puncture reorderings and marker rotations is still denoted
G(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
.

The above uniform choice of
{\bf q}
helps stabilize the automorphism group of
(\mathcal{F},u)
and the following analog of Lemma
3.6.2
holds:

Lemma 4.2.11
.

The stabilizer
\operatorname{Mod}(F,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})_{[j]}
is trivial.

Fix
(F,j,{\bf p})
. (This is equivalent to choosing a Teichmüller slice of
\operatorname{Teich}(\dot{F})
, since
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
.) Let
K_{0}
be a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}_{J}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
. The following is a slight variation of the definition that was given for
\chi(\dot{F})<0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{K_{0}}=\mathcal{B}_{K_{0}}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,\mathbf{p},{\bf q},\mathbf{r}),u)\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}u\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}),\\
u\mbox{ is $\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$-close to $u^{\prime}$ where $[u^{\prime}]\in K_{0}$},\\
u(r_{\pm,i})=x_{\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}},{\bf q}\mbox{ satisfies \ref{Q}}\end{array}\right.\right\},

where
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
is a sufficiently small constant that depends on
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
K_{0}
, and
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
-closeness is measured with respect to the norm on
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},(u^{\prime})^{*}D_{\epsilon})
(we choose
k,p
as in Remark
4.2.3
to ensure
u
is
C^{1}
-close to
u^{\prime}
so we can apply Lemma
4.2.8
or
4.2.9
).
As before, when the Teichmüller slice, i.e., the choice of
(F,j,{\bf p})
, and the compact subset
K_{0}
are understood, they will often be omitted from the notation, e.g.,
\overline{\partial}_{J}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{E}
.

The fibers
\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
of the bundle
\pi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{B}
are defined by

\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})

as before and
\overline{\partial}_{J}
is the same as before. The linearization
L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
is the same as that of Equation (
4.2.4
) with the term
T\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(j)
removed (or viewed as the zero vector space).

4.3.
Description of the linearized
\overline{\partial}
-operator
D_{u}

By McDuff-Salamon
[
MS
, Proposition 3.1.1]
,

D_{u}:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})\to W^{k-1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})

is given by

(4.3.1)

D_{u}\xi=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\xi+J(u)\nabla\xi\circ j)-\frac{1}{2}J(u)(\nabla_{\xi}J)(u)\partial_{J}(u).

Here, by abuse of notation, we do not distinguish between sections of
u^{*}T\widehat{W}
and sections of
T\widehat{W}
along
u
.

Suppose
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}
is a holomorphic coordinate around a puncture
p\in{\bf p}_{\pm}
of
\dot{F}
,
p
corresponds to the Reeb orbit
\gamma
, and
u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t))
, written in simple coordinates for
\gamma
, is
J
-holomorphic. Then, near this puncture,

(4.3.2)

D_{u}\xi=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{s}\xi+J(u)\nabla_{t}\xi+\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}\xi)\otimes(ds-idt),

where

J(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
X\eta&-j_{0}X\eta&j_{0}\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and }\quad\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&-j_{0}X\end{array}\right).

Note that if
u
is
J
-holomorphic, then
D_{u}u_{s}=D_{u}u_{t}=0
. If, instead of using the basis

\{\partial_{s},\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\},

we use the basis

\{u_{s},u_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\},

then Equation (
4.3.2
) becomes

(4.3.3)

D_{u}\xi=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{s}\xi+J_{0}\partial_{t}\xi+\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}\xi)\otimes(ds-idt),

where

J_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&j_{0}\end{array}\right)

is the standard complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+2}
.

Note that
\partial_{x_{i}},\partial_{y_{i}}
is not a continuous vector field on
[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\times D_{\delta}^{2\overline{m}}\times D_{\delta}^{2\underline{m}}/_{\sim}
due to the identification
\sim
, but it does not affect the matrix representation of
\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}
and
J_{0}.

The vector field
\xi=(\xi^{\circ},\overline{\xi},\underline{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\overline{m}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\underline{m}}
in Equation (
4.3.3
) needs to satisfy the
\xi^{\circ}(s,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=\xi^{\circ}(s,0),\overline{\xi}(s,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=\overline{\xi}(s,0)
, and
\underline{\xi}(s,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=-\underline{\xi}(s,0)
.

If
u
is immersed, we denote by
N
the normal bundle of
u
such that

N=\mathbb{R}\langle\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{2n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{2n}}\rangle

near the ends. We define

D^{N}_{u}:W_{\delta}^{k,p}(\dot{F},u^{*}N)\to W_{\delta}^{k-1,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}N)

by projecting
D_{u}
along the splitting
u^{*}T\widehat{W}=u^{*}N\oplus T\dot{F}
.

4.4.
The asymptotic operator

Let
\gamma
be a Reeb orbit of
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha}
.
We denote by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
the space of functions
f=(f^{\circ},\overline{f},\underline{f}):[0,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\overline{m}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\underline{m}}
that satisfies
f^{\circ}(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=f^{\circ}(0)
,
\overline{f}(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=\overline{f}(0)
and
\underline{f}(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma))=-\underline{f}(0).

Let
W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}})
and
L^{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}})
be the completion of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
under
W^{1,2}
and
L^{2}
-norms respectively.
Let

\widetilde{A}^{\gamma}=-J_{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}:W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}})\to L^{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}})

be the asymptotic operator for
\gamma
. Then
\widetilde{A}^{\gamma}
is self-adjoint.
Let

\dots\leq\lambda_{-2}^{\gamma}<\lambda_{-1}^{\gamma}=0=\lambda_{1}^{\gamma}<\lambda_{2}^{\gamma}\leq\dots

be eigenvalues of
\widetilde{A}^{\gamma}
and

\dots,f_{-2}^{\gamma},f_{-1}^{\gamma},f_{1}^{\gamma},f_{2}^{\gamma},\dots

be the associated complete set of orthonormal eigenfunctions.

Here the self-adjointness and orthonormality are defined with respect to the standard inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+2}
;
the two zero eigenvalues
\lambda_{-1}^{\gamma}
and
\lambda_{1}^{\gamma}
correspond to the eigenfunctions
(1,0,0,\dots,0)
and
(0,1,0,\dots,0)
; the fact that all other eigenfunctions are non-zero follows from the non-degenerency of
\gamma
(see
[
BH
]
for a calculation of eigenfunctions in dimension three).

We will write
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{S}
for
\widetilde{A}^{\gamma}
and
\widetilde{S}^{\gamma}
if
\gamma
is understood; similarly we may suppress the superscript
\gamma
in
\lambda_{i}^{\gamma},f_{i}^{\gamma},\dots
below, when they are understood.

Remark 4.4.1
.

\widetilde{A}
is an “enlargement” of the operator
A:W^{1,2}(\mathfrak{C})\to L^{2}(\mathfrak{C})
in the proof of Lemma
3.5.3
which takes into account only the transverse directions (i.e., the
\partial_{x_{i}}
- and
\partial_{y_{i}}
-directions).

Summarizing the above discussion, we have:

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Suppose
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}
is a holomorphic coordinate around a puncture
p\in{\bf p}_{\pm}
of
\dot{F}
,
p
corresponds to the Reeb orbit
\gamma
, and near
p
,
u
admits a lift to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}\times D
which can be written as
(s,t)\mapsto(s,t,\eta(s,t))
with respect to simple coordinates for
\gamma
and is
J
-holomorphic. Then, near
p
,

D_{u}\xi=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{s}\xi-\widetilde{A}\xi)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(ds-idt),

with respect to the basis

\mathfrak{B}:=\{u_{s},u_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\}.

Hence, for any
\xi\in\ker D_{u}
, near
p\in{\bf p}_{+}
we have

\xi(s,t)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}<0}c_{+,i}e^{\lambda_{i}s}f_{i}(t),

and near
p\in{\bf p}_{-}

we have

\xi(s,t)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}>0}c_{-,i}e^{\lambda_{i}s}f_{i}(t),

where the
c_{\pm,i}
’s are constant.

4.5.
The adjoint operator

Let

D_{u}^{*}:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})\to W^{k-1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})

be the adjoint operator of
D_{u}
defined by

(4.5.1)

\int_{\dot{F}}\langle\zeta,D_{u}\xi\rangle_{1}dvol_{\dot{F}}=\int_{\dot{F}}\langle D_{u}^{*}\zeta,\xi\rangle_{0}dvol_{\dot{F}},

where
\langle,\rangle_{1}
is an inner product on
W_{\delta}^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})
,
\langle,\rangle_{0}
is an inner product on
W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})
, and
dvol_{\dot{F}}
is defined using a metric
g_{\dot{F}}
on
\dot{F}
. We additionally assume that, near
p\in{\bf p}_{\pm}
:

(i)

g_{\dot{F}}
restricts to
ds^{2}+dt^{2}
;

(ii)

\langle,\rangle_{0}
is defined by requiring the basis
{\mathfrak{B}}
to be an orthonormal basis; and

(iii)

\langle,\rangle_{1}
is induced by
\langle,\rangle_{0}
and
g_{\dot{F}}
.

Remark 4.5.1
.

Recall that the adjoint operator
D_{u}^{*}
involve some choices. We are not using the Riemannian metric
g_{0}
in the definition of
D_{u}^{*}
.

The following lemma is almost evident:

Lemma 4.5.2
.

With the assumptions of Lemma
4.4.2
, if
\zeta\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})
and
\zeta=\zeta^{0}\otimes(ds-idt)
near
p\in{\bf p}_{\pm}
, then we have

D_{u}^{*}(\zeta^{0}\otimes(ds-idt))=\frac{1}{2}(-\partial_{s}\zeta^{0}-\widetilde{A}\zeta^{0}),

with respect to the basis
{\mathfrak{B}}
. Hence, for any
\zeta\in\ker D_{u}^{*}
, near
p\in{\bf p}_{+}
we have

\zeta^{0}(s,t)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}>0}c_{+,i}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}f_{i}(t),

whereas near
p\in{\bf p}_{-}
we have

\zeta^{0}(s,t)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}<0}c_{-,i}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}f_{i}(t),

where the
c_{\pm,i}
’s are constant.

5.
Interior semi-global Kuranishi charts

Consider the moduli space

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{J}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})

and the finite group
G=G(\dot{F},\widehat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
. As discussed in Section
3.5
, there are two slightly different cases to consider: (Symp) and
\operatorname{(Cob)}
.

The goal of this section is to construct a tuple

(K,\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},\psi,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}G),

called an
interior semi-global Kuranishi chart
. In the (Symp) case this consists of:

(I1)

a compact subset
K\subset\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
;

(I2)

a finite rank bundle
\pi_{V}:E\to V
over a finite-dimensional manifold, called an
obstruction bundle
;

(I3)

a section
\overline{\partial}_{J}:V\to E
;

(I4)

a homeomorphism
\psi:\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(0)\to\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
onto an open subset of
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
and
K\subset\operatorname{Im}(\psi)
;

(I5)

\dim V-\operatorname{rk}E=\operatorname{vdim}\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
; and

(I6)

K
is invariant under the finite group
G
, the action of
G
extends to an action on
\pi_{V}:E\to V
by bundle automorphisms, and the quotient by
G
gives a global quotient orbibundle (i.e., can be described by a single orbibundle chart).

In the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case,
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
is replaced by
\mathcal{M}
.

In practice, we take
K
to be “large” in the sense that the only curves that are excluded are very close to breaking. This will be made more precise later, when we explain the notions of close to breaking and neck length. In particular, if
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
is compact, then we take
K=\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
.

We will also construct a multisection
\mathfrak{s}
of the bundle
E\to V
, called the
obstruction multisection
, such that
\mathfrak{s}\pitchfork\overline{\partial}_{J}
.

5.1.
Construction of
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse subbundles

Suppressing the Teichmüller slice
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
or the compact subset
K_{0}
from the notation,

consider the Banach bundle
\pi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{B}
and the section
\overline{\partial}_{J}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{E}
corresponding to the quadruple
(\dot{F},\widehat{W},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
, as defined in

Section
4.2.2
for the case
\chi(\dot{F})<0
and Section
4.2.5
for the case
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
. Let
Z=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(0)
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a subset of
\mathcal{B}
.
A subbundle
E\to\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{V}}
is
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse
if
E
has finite rank and, for each
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{V}
,

(5.1.1)

\operatorname{Im}L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}+E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)},

where
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
refer to the fibers of
E
and
\mathcal{E}
over
(\mathcal{F},u)
. Here
+
in Equation (
5.1.1
) means span and
does not mean direct sum.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following:

Theorem 5.1.2
.

Given a compact subset
K
of
Z
, there exist a positive integer
\ell_{0}
and a sufficiently small open neighborhood
\mathcal{N}(K)\subset\mathcal{B}
of
K
such that for all
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, there exists a
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse rank
\ell(l_{+}+l_{-})
subbundle

E\to\mathcal{N}(K)

of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}
satisfying:

(1)

if
(\mathcal{F},u),(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}(K)
and there exists a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism
\phi:(\mathcal{F},u)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
, then there is a canonical identification
\phi_{*}E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=E_{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u\circ\phi^{-1})}
which is induced from

\phi_{*}:\wedge^{0,1}T\dot{F}\otimes u^{*}T\widehat{W}\xrightarrow{\sim}\wedge^{0,1}T\dot{F}\otimes(u\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}T\widehat{W};

(2)

if
(\widehat{W}=\mathbb{R}\times M,J)\in\operatorname{(Symp)}
and
(\mathcal{F},u),(\mathcal{F},u_{T})\in\mathcal{N}(K)
, where
u_{T}
is
u
translated up by
s=T
, i.e.,
u_{T}=\Phi_{T}\circ u
where

\Phi_{T}:\mathbb{R}\times M\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}\times M,\quad(s,x)\mapsto(s+T,x)

is the
s=T
translation map, then
(\Phi_{T})_{*}E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=E_{(\mathcal{F},u_{T})}
.

Remark 5.1.3
.

When
\operatorname{dim}(M)=3
, it is possible to obtain an effective bound on
\ell_{0}
using the positivity of intersections and winding numbers (see
[
HLS
,
HWZ2
,
We
]
).

Remark 5.1.4
.

In addition to
\ell
, the bundle
E\to\mathcal{N}(K)
also depends on
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K)>0
small satisfying (i) and (ii) in Step 1A when
\chi(\dot{F})<0
and (i) and (ii) with
\dot{F}
replaced by
\ddot{F}
when
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
. The dependence on
\epsilon
and
\ell
will be discussed in Section
5.4
.

Proof.

The proof consists of three steps. Let
g_{(\dot{F},j)}
(resp.
g_{(\ddot{F},j)}
) be the complete finite volume hyperbolic metric on
\dot{F}
(resp.
\ddot{F}=\dot{F}-{\bf q}
) compatible with
j
, when
\chi(\dot{F})<0
(resp.
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
).

Step 1.
(Definition of
s_{\pm,i}
) Let
K
be a compact subset of
Z
.
The goal of this step is to define continuous maps
s_{\pm,i}:K\to\mathbb{R}

with differentiable extensions
s_{\pm,i}:\mathcal{N}(K)\to\mathbb{R}
, where the differentiability depends on the choice of
W^{k+1,p}
. Here each
s_{\pm,i}
corresponds to
\gamma_{\pm,i}
and
\mathcal{N}(K)
is a small open neighborhood of
K
which we may shrink as we go along.

The collection
\{s_{\pm,i}\}
will be invariant under group actions as follows:

(a)

If
(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u)\sim(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})=((F,j^{\prime},{\bf p},{\bf q}^{\prime},{\bf r}^{\prime}),u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}(K)
via the automorphism
\phi:\dot{F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\dot{F}
, then
\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)\}=\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})\}
.

(b)

If
(\mathcal{F},u),(\mathcal{F},u_{T})\in\mathcal{N}(K)
, then
\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u_{T})\}=\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)+T\}
.

Let
\kappa>0
be a predetermined constant which is smaller than
1/100
the radii of all the simple coordinate charts of all the orbits used in
(\mathcal{F},u)
(and is independent of
K
).

Step 1A.
Suppose
\chi(\dot{F})<0
. There exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K)>0
small such that, for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in K
,

(i)

the
\varepsilon
-thin part
\operatorname{Thin}_{\varepsilon}(\dot{F},g_{(\dot{F},j)})
of
(\dot{F},g_{(\dot{F},j)})
, is the disjoint union of cusps

(5.1.2)

C_{+,1},\dots,C_{+,l_{+}},C_{-,1},\dots,C_{-,l_{-}}

and possibly some annuli (of moduli
\geq 0
), where each
\partial C_{\pm,i}
is a circle which corresponds to
\gamma_{\pm,i}
and

(ii)

\operatorname{Im}(u|_{C_{\pm,i}})
is
\kappa
-close to
[s_{+,i},\infty)\times\gamma_{+,i}^{s}
or
(-\infty,s_{-,i}]\times\gamma_{-,i}^{s}
for some
s_{\pm,i}
.

Here the distance is measured with respect to the Hausdorff metric derived from the Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
\widehat{W}
which is
s
-invariant at the ends. The existence of
\varepsilon>0
satisfying (i) for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in K
is standard hyperbolic geometry and the existence of
\varepsilon>0
satisfying (ii) for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in K
relies on the exponential decay estimates of the ends of
u
from
[
HWZ1
]
. Observe that (i) and (ii) also hold for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
, provided
\mathcal{N}(K)
is a sufficiently small neighborhood of
K
.

We then choose a specific
s_{\pm,i}=s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)
as follows: First consider the
m(\gamma_{\pm,i})
-fold covering map

\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i})\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s})\mathbb{Z}
. Using canonical cylindrical coordinates, we write
u
on
C_{\pm,i}
as

u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t)),

after lifting to the
m(\gamma_{\pm,i})
-fold cover
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i})\mathbb{Z})\times D
, subject to the condition that
t=0
corresponds to the asymptotic marker
r_{\pm,i}
.

Definition 5.1.5

(
s_{\pm,i}
and
c_{\pm,i}
)
.

The average of the
s
-coordinates of the points on
u|_{\partial C_{\pm,i}}
with
t=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}),2\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i}^{s}),\dots,\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\pm,i})
will be denoted by
s_{\pm,i}
. Also
the point on
\partial C_{\pm,i}
corresponding to
r_{\pm,i}
will be denoted by
c_{\pm,i}
.

Step 1B.
Suppose
\chi(\dot{F})=0
or
1
. In view of the discussion from Section
4.2.5
(with
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
satisfying Lemmas
4.2.8
and
4.2.9
), we take
\ddot{F}=\dot{F}-{\bf q}
with
\chi(\ddot{F})<0
. Let
g=g_{(\ddot{F},j)}
and apply the considerations from Step 1A with
\dot{F}
replaced by
\ddot{F}
to obtain
s_{\pm,i}=s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)
.

(a) and (b) are immediate from the construction, both in Steps 1A and 1B.

Remark 5.1.6

(Trivial cylinders)
.

We do not construct Kuranishi charts for trivial cylinders when
(\widehat{W},J)
is in the (Symp) case. On the other hand, when a symplectization is viewed in
\operatorname{(Cob)}
, our general prescription is to slightly perturb the almost complex structure using Lemma
3.5.3
so that the perturbed trivial cylinders no longer have trivial ends. We note that trivial (possibly multiply-covered) cylinders are automatically transverse.

Step 2.
(Definition of
E
) Let
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K)
be the constant from Step 1.

Let
\beta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a nondecreasing smooth cutoff function such that
\beta(s)=0
for
s\leq 0
and
\beta(s)=1
for
s\geq 1
and let
\beta^{+}_{s_{0}}(s)=\beta(s-s_{0})
and
\beta^{-}_{s_{0}}(s)=-\beta(s_{0}-s)
, where
s_{0}>0
is a constant.

Given
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
and
s_{\pm,i}=s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)
from Step 1 (and still using canonical cylindrical coordinates), let

(5.1.3)

\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{j}(s,t):=\frac{\partial\beta^{\pm}_{s_{\pm,i}}}{\partial s}(s)f^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{j}(t)\otimes\pi_{j}(ds-idt)\in\Gamma(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W}).

Here
\pi_{j}:W^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\dot{F})\to W^{k,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}T^{*}\dot{F})
is the projection with respect to the domain complex structure and
\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{j}(s,t)
has support on a single end of
\dot{F}
.
We then define

(5.1.4)

E_{(\mathcal{F},u),{\gamma_{\pm,i}}}^{\ell,\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{R}\langle\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{\ell}\rangle

for
{\gamma_{\pm,i}}\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
and define

(5.1.5)

E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon}:=\bigoplus_{{\gamma_{\pm,i}}\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}\cup\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}}E_{(\mathcal{F},u),{\gamma_{\pm,i}}}^{\ell,\varepsilon}.

This gives us a bundle
E:=E^{\ell,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
with fiber
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon}
of dimension
\ell(l_{+}+l_{-})
. The following will be proven in the Appendix:

Lemma 5.1.7
.

E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}\subset\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
and
E\to\mathcal{N}(K)
is of class
C^{1}
if
k\geq 3
and
p>2
.

Step 3.
(
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transversality) We first prove the following transversality lemma, which is applicable for a single
(\mathcal{F},u)\in K
.

Lemma 5.1.8

(Transversality lemma)
.

For each
(\mathcal{F},u)\in K
, there exist
\ell_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

(5.1.6)

E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{{\ell,\varepsilon}}+\operatorname{Im}D_{u}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\wedge^{0,1}\dot{F}\otimes u^{*}T\widehat{W})

for all
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
and
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Remark 5.1.9
.

Observe that we are actually proving a slightly stronger statement than

E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{{\ell,\varepsilon}}+\operatorname{Im}L_{(\mathcal{F},u)}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\wedge^{0,1}\dot{F}\otimes u^{*}T\widehat{W}).

Proof.

We first claim that, if Equation (
5.1.6
) does not hold, then there exists a nonzero
\zeta\in\ker D_{u}^{*}
which is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon}
. To see this, consider the projection map
\pi:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\wedge^{0,1}\dot{F}\otimes u^{*}T\widehat{W})=\ker D_{u}^{*}\oplus\operatorname{Im}D_{u}\to\ker D_{u}^{*}
. If Equation (
5.1.6
) does not hold, then
\pi(E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon})
cannot be all of
\ker D_{u}^{*}
. Taking
\zeta\in\ker D_{u}^{*}
to be nonzero and orthogonal to
\pi(E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon})
, we have
\zeta\perp E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell,\varepsilon}
since
\zeta\perp\operatorname{Im}D_{u}
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small so that on the
\varepsilon_{0}
-thin neighborhood of a puncture of
\dot{F}
corresponding to the Reeb orbit
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
(
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
is similar),
\zeta\in\ker D_{u}^{*}
can be written with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}
from Lemmas
4.4.2
and
4.5.2
as

\zeta(s,t)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}>0}c_{i}e^{-\lambda_{i}^{\gamma}s}f_{i}^{\gamma}(t)\otimes(ds-idt),

and let
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. The key point is that, when
\varepsilon_{0}
is sufficiently small,
\mathfrak{B}
is arbitrarily close to
\{\partial_{s},\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\}
and, without loss of generality, we may assume
\zeta(s,t)
is written with respect to the latter. There exists a basis
\{\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k}\}
for
\ker D_{u}^{*}
for which the coefficients
c_{ji}
(this means
c_{i}
for
\zeta_{j}
) form a matrix in row echelon form; in particular, there are no rows with all
c_{ji}=0
since the corresponding
\zeta_{j}
would be identically zero by unique continuation, which is a contradiction.

Let
\ell_{0}>0
be larger than the smallest
i
for which
c_{ki}\not=0
. Now observe that if
\zeta\in\ker D_{u}^{*}
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{\ell_{0},\varepsilon}
, then its coefficients
c_{i}=0
for all
i\leq\ell_{0}
, since the
L^{2}
-inner product of
\zeta
and
\widetilde{f}_{i}^{\gamma}
is nonzero if
c_{i}\not=0
. This implies that
\zeta=0
. The lemma then follows.
∎

Lemma
5.1.8
can be improved as follows:

Lemma 5.1.10

(Family transversality lemma)
.

There exist
\ell_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and a sufficiently small open neighborhood
\mathcal{N}(K)\subset\mathcal{B}
of
K
, such that

E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}^{{\ell,\varepsilon}}+\operatorname{Im}D_{u}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\wedge^{0,1}\dot{F}\otimes u^{*}T\widehat{W}).

for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
,
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, and
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
5.1.8
, the compactness of
K
, and the following well-known property of Fredholm maps: Let
L,L^{\prime}:V\to W
be Fredholm maps between Banach spaces. If
L
and
L^{\prime}
are close in the operator norm and
\operatorname{Im}(L)+W^{\prime}=W
for a finite-dimensional subspace
W^{\prime}
, then
\operatorname{Im}(L^{\prime})+W^{\prime}=W
.

For
(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0}),(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\in K
that are close, we identify the Banach spaces involved in
L_{(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})}
,
i=0,1
, by defining an isomorphism
\mathcal{P}:u_{0}^{*}T\widehat{W}\xrightarrow{\sim}u_{1}^{*}T\widehat{W}
as follows:

(i)

on a large compact region of
\dot{F}
,
\mathcal{P}
is given by parallel translation along the shortest geodesics between
u_{0}(z)
and
u_{1}(z)
;

(ii)

on the ends of
\dot{F}
(assumed to be sufficiently small),
\mathcal{P}
is given by identifying the bases

\{(u_{0})_{s},(u_{0})_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\}

\{(u_{1})_{s},(u_{1})_{t},\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{y_{1}},\dots,\partial_{y_{n}}\};

(iii)

\mathcal{P}
interpolates between (i) and (ii) in the intermediate region.

Then
L_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
and
L_{(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})}
are close in the operator norm; the details are left to the reader.
∎

This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
.
∎

5.2.
The obstruction bundles
\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V

Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
in the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case and
\mathcal{M}
in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case.

5.2.1.
Definition of
\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V

With the bundle
E=E^{\ell,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
constructed in Theorem
5.1.2
, we define

(5.2.1)

V:=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(E)\subset\mathcal{N}(K).

Since
E
is
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse and of class
C^{1}
,
V\supset K
is a finite-dimensional submanifold of
\mathcal{N}(K)
of class
C^{1}
. Once we have extracted
V
, since the elements of
V
are smooth by elliptic regularity,
V
can be “upgraded to” a smooth finite-dimensional manifold of the expected dimension.

Observe that if
(\mathcal{F},u)\in V
, then
u:\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
is a smooth map whose ends are
J
-holomorphic and asymptotic to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}\cup\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
. Let

(5.2.2)

\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V

be the restriction of
E\to\mathcal{N}(K)
to
V
and let
\overline{\partial}_{J}:V\to E|_{V}
be the restriction of
\overline{\partial}_{J}
to
V
. The vector bundle
\pi_{V}
is a smooth vector bundle over
V
.

5.2.2.
Patching together the bundles
\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V

We would like to “patch together” the obstruction bundles
\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V
so that (I1)–(I6) are satisfied. To this end we prove Lemmas
5.2.1
,
5.2.3
, and
5.2.4
below, which can roughly be stated as:

(1)

independence of the choice of Teichmüller slice;

(2)

equivariance under puncture reorderings and marker rotations; and

(3)

invariance under
\mathbb{R}
-translations in the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case.

Recall that
\operatorname{Aut}(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})=1
by Lemmas
3.6.2
and
4.2.11
, so there are no nontrivial domain automorphisms.

We first consider (1). Suppose we are in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case; an analogous result holds in the (Symp) case, but will not be stated explicitly. In the
\chi(\dot{F})<0
case let
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
be two Teichmüller slices of
\mathcal{U}\subset\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{Teich}(F,{\bf p},{\bf r})
and let
\phi_{x}:F\xrightarrow{\sim}F
,
x\in\mathcal{U}
, be the smooth family of diffeomorphisms taking
j_{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
to
j^{\prime}_{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
from Section
4.2.3
. We take bundles
\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}:\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}\to\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}
and compact subsets
K_{i}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}
which are identified with
K
. In the
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
case taking a Teichmüller slice of
\operatorname{Teich}(\dot{F})
is equivalent to fixing
(F,j,{\bf p})
. Writing
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}}=\{(F,j_{i},{\bf p})\}
for
i=0,1
, we have a diffeomorphism
\phi
taking
j_{0}
to
j_{1}
, and we take bundles
\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}},K_{i}}:\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}},K_{i}}\to\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}},K_{i}}
where the
K_{i}
are identified with
K
.

Let
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
,
i=0,1
, be the bundle
\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V
extracted from the Banach bundles
\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}
or
\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}},K_{i}}
as appropriate, using Theorem
5.1.2
.

Lemma 5.2.1

(First patching lemma)
.

Suppose we are in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case. After possibly shrinking
V_{i}
subject to the condition
V_{i}\supset K_{i}
,
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are diffeomorphic and the bundles
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
are isomorphic and the identifications only depend on the family of diffeomorphisms
\{\phi_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{U}}
in the
\chi(\dot{F})<0
case or
\phi
in the
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
case.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r}),u)\in V_{0}
,
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})=((F,j^{\prime},\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}),u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}(K_{1})
, and
\phi
be the diffeomorphism satisfying

\phi_{*}((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u)=((F,j^{\prime},{\bf p},{\bf q}^{\prime},{\bf r}^{\prime}),u^{\prime}).

Then
\phi
induces a natural identification
\phi_{*}E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}\simeq E_{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})}
provided

E_{i}=E_{i}^{\ell,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{N}(K_{i}),\quad i=0,1

are constructed as in the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
with fixed
\ell
and
\varepsilon
. This is due to the fact that, in addition to
\ell
and
\varepsilon
,
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
and
\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)\}
(and likewise
E_{(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})}
and
\{s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})\}
) only depend on the image of
u
and the hyperbolic geometry (i.e., the thick-thin decomposition) of
g_{(\dot{F},j)}
. In particular,
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},u^{\prime})\in V_{1}
, provided
(\mathcal{F},u)\in V_{0}
is sufficiently close to
K_{0}
.

The family of diffeomorphisms
\{\phi_{x}\},x\in\mathcal{U}
, in the
\chi(\dot{F})<0
case or the diffeomorphism
\phi
in the
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
case gives a smooth map
\Phi
from
E_{0}|_{V_{0}}\to V_{0}
to
E_{1}|_{V_{1}}\to V_{1}
, after possibly shrinking
V_{0}
. Since
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism onto its image and the image of
V_{0}
contains
K_{1}
, by the invariance of domain,
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism
E_{0}|_{V_{0}}\to V_{0}
to
E_{1}|_{V_{1}}\to V_{1}
, after possibly shrinking
V_{1}
.
∎

Remark 5.2.2
.

We pass to
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
at an early stage to avoid the well-known “loss of derivative” issue when dealing with Sobolev spaces. In particular, we avoid identifying
E_{0}\to\mathcal{N}(K_{0})
and
E_{1}\to\mathcal{N}(K_{1})
.

(2) is stated more precisely as follows: Suppose we are in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case. Let
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{[j]}
be a neighborhood of
[j]
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
be a Teichmüller slice of
\mathcal{U}
containing
j
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{1}=g(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{0})
is a Teichmüller slice of
g(\mathcal{U})
, where
g
is a composition of puncture reorderings and marker rotations. Let
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
,
i=0,1
, be bundles constructed using
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
and neighborhoods of
K_{i}
, where
K_{1}=g(K_{0})
.

Lemma 5.2.3

(Second patching lemma)
.

Suppose we are in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case. After possibly shrinking
V_{i}
subject to the condition
V_{i}\supset K_{i}
,
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are diffeomorphic via the isomorphism
g
and the bundles
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Similar to that of Lemma
5.2.1
and relies on the fact that
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
and
E_{g(\mathcal{F},u)}
are naturally isomorphic.
∎

(3) is stated more precisely as follows:

Lemma 5.2.4

(Third patching lemma)
.

Suppose we are in the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case and
K_{i}
,
i=0,1
, are compact subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i},K_{i}}
and
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K_{0})
if and only if
(\mathcal{F},u_{T})\in\mathcal{N}(K_{1})
, where
u_{T}
is
u
translated up by
s=T
. Then, after possibly shrinking
V_{i}
subject to the condition
V_{i}\supset K_{i}
, the bundles
\pi_{V_{i}}:E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}
are canonically isomorphic.

Proof.

Similar to that of Lemma
5.2.1
.
∎

5.3.
Interior semi-global Kuranishi chart

In this subsection we construct an interior semi-global Kuranishi chart

(K,\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},\psi,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}G})

and an obstruction multisection
{\mathfrak{s}}
, where
K
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
in the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case and
\mathcal{M}
in the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case, and
G=G(\dot{F},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-})
.

5.3.1.
Construction of the chart

We will treat the
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case; the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case analogous but simpler since there is no target
\mathbb{R}
-translation.

Let
K
be a
G
-invariant compact subset of
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
and let
K_{0}
be the lift of
K
to a
G
-invariant slice of
\mathcal{M}
under the
\mathbb{R}
-action. In what follows we will not distinguish between
K
and
K_{0}
.

Proposition 5.3.1
.

There exists a tuple
(K,\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},\psi,G)
such that the rank of
\pi_{V}
depends on
\ell_{0}\gg 0
and (I1)–(I6) hold.

Proof.

When
\chi(\dot{F})<0
, let
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}\to\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
be the Banach bundle for some Teichmüller slice
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
\overline{\partial}_{J}:\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
be the
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-section. When
\chi(\dot{F})\geq 0
, the Banach bundle
\mathcal{E}_{K_{0}}\to\mathcal{B}_{K_{0}}
is defined using
K_{0}
.

In either case Theorem
5.1.2
gives a
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse subbundle
E_{0}\to\mathcal{N}(K_{0})
. If we set
V_{0}=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(E_{0})
, then
\pi_{V_{0}}:E_{0}|_{V_{0}}\to V_{0}
is a finite rank vector bundle over a finite-dimensional manifold. Note that
\pi_{V_{0}}:E_{0}|_{V_{0}}\to V_{0}
is independent of the choice of Teichmüller slice by Lemma
5.2.1
. Next we shrink
V_{0}
subject to
V_{0}\supset K_{0}
so that
\pi_{V_{0}}:E_{0}|_{V_{0}}\to V_{0}
is invariant under
G
. Let
V
be the quotient of
V_{0}
by
\mathbb{R}
-translations of the target and let
E
be the quotient of
E_{0}
by the induced
\mathbb{R}
-action, i.e.,
((\mathcal{F},u),\zeta)\sim^{\prime}((\mathcal{F},u_{T}),\zeta_{T})
, where
u_{T}
is
u
translated up by
s=T
and
\zeta_{T}
is
\zeta
translated up by
s=T
.
The quotient exists by Lemma
5.2.4
. (I1), (I2), (I3), and (I5) follow immediately.

(I4) The map
\psi:\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(0)\to\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
induced by
(\mathcal{F},u)\mapsto(\mathcal{F},u)
is a homeomorphism onto the image in view of the discussion of the topology of
\mathcal{M}
from Section
4.2.3
.

(I6) By construction
K
is invariant under the finite group
G
. The action of
G
extends to an action on
\pi_{V}:E\to V
by bundle automorphisms by Lemma
5.2.3
. The quotient by
G
gives a global quotient orbibundle after verifying a couple of facts.

(a) If
\ell_{0}
is sufficiently large, then the action of
G
on
V
is effective: Let
g\not=e\in G
and suppose
g(\mathcal{F},u)=(\mathcal{F},u)
. If
\ell_{0}
is sufficiently large, then there exists
(\mathcal{F},u^{\prime})\in V
that is close to
(\mathcal{F},u)
and such that
\overline{\partial}_{J}(\mathcal{F},u^{\prime})=\zeta
and
\zeta
is not fixed under
g
: Start with
\ell_{1}
sufficiently large so that
E^{\ell_{1}}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
is
D_{u}
-transverse. Then there exists
\zeta_{0}\in E^{\ell_{1}+1}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
with nontrivial
\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{\ell_{1}+1}
component for which there exists
\xi_{0}
satisfying
D_{u}\xi_{0}=\zeta_{0}
. When
\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{\ell_{1}+1}
is not invariant under
g
(it is easy to find such an
\ell_{1}
and
\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{\ell_{1}+1}
),
\xi_{0}
is also not invariant under
g
. Passing from infinitesimal to local, there exists
(\mathcal{F},u^{\prime})
near
(\mathcal{F},u)
that is not invariant under
g
when
\ell_{0}\geq\ell_{1}+1
.

(a) in turn implies that the action of
G
on
E
is effective.

(b)
V
is Hausdorff: This is a consequence of the standard fact that
V=V_{0}/G
,
V_{0}
is Hausdorff, and
G
is a finite group.
∎

5.3.2.
Construction of
{\mathfrak{s}}

Let
s
be a generic smooth section of
E\to V
which is transverse to
\overline{\partial}_{J}
and is a perturbation of the zero section. Taking the collection
(g(s))_{g\in G}
where we assign the weight
1/|G|
to each branch, we obtain a smooth
lifted
multisection
{\mathfrak{s}}
of the global quotient orbifold for
\pi_{V}:E\to V
which is a perturbation of the zero (multi-)section and such that
{\mathfrak{s}}\pitchfork\overline{\partial}_{J}
.

5.4.
Dependence on
\varepsilon
and
\ell

In this subsection we briefly discuss the effect of increasing
\ell>0
and shrinking
\varepsilon>0
.

5.4.1.
Stabilizations

Definition 5.4.1
.

Let
E\to\mathcal{N}(K)
be a
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse subbundle of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}
. A subbundle
E^{\prime}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}
such that
E\subset E^{\prime}
is a
rank
a
stabilization of
E
if for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
there is a vector bundle isomorphism
E\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}\xrightarrow{\sim}E^{\prime}
that takes
(x,0)\mapsto x
.

An example of a stabilization of
E^{\ell,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
is
E^{\ell+1,\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
.

Definition 5.4.2
.

The pair
(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime})
consisting of a finite rank vector bundle
\pi_{V^{\prime}}
over a finite-dimensional manifold and a section
\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime}
of
\pi_{V^{\prime}}
is a
rank
a
stabilization of
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J})
if

(1)

there is a splitting
E^{\prime}\simeq E^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}
and a bundle map

(5.4.1)

\begin{diagram}

where
i:V\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}
is an inclusion and
j:E\to E^{\prime\prime}|_{i(V)}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles; and

(2)

i:V\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}
extends to a diffeomorphism

(5.4.2)

i:V\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B^{a}}\xrightarrow{\sim}V^{\prime}

so that
\overline{\partial}^{\prime}_{J}(i(v,x))=(j(\overline{\partial}_{J}(v)),x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, where
x
is viewed both as an element of an open Euclidean ball
B^{a}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{a}~|~|x|<1\}
and as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{a}
.

Given a stabilization
E^{\prime}
of
E\to\mathcal{N}(K)
, by setting
V=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(E)
and
V^{\prime}=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(E^{\prime})
, we obtain a stabilization
(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}|_{V^{\prime}}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J})
of
(\pi_{V}:E|_{V}\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J})
. Note that Definition
5.4.2
(2) holds after shrinking
V^{\prime}
subject to
V^{\prime}\supset K
if necessary.

We now consider triples
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},G)
consisting of a global quotient orbibundle
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,G)
and a section
\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime}
of the orbibundle.

Definition 5.4.3
.

The triple
(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime},G)
is a
rank
a
stabilization of
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},G)

if

(1)

(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime})
is a rank
a
stabilization of
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J})
;

(2)

all the maps in (
5.4.1
) and (
5.4.2
) are
G
-equivariant;

(3)

the splitting
E^{\prime}\simeq E^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}
from Definition
5.4.2
(1) is not necessarily a direct sum decomposition of
G
-bundles; and

(4)

given
v\in V
and
g\in G
,
g
induces maps

\displaystyle\{v\}\times B^{a}\to\{g(v)\}\times B^{a},\quad(v,x)\mapsto(g(v),L^{1}_{g,v}(x)),

\displaystyle E^{\prime\prime}_{v}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}\to E^{\prime\prime}_{g(v)}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a},\quad(y,x)\mapsto(L^{0}_{g,v}(y),L^{1}_{g,v}(x)),

where
L^{0}_{g,v}
is a linear map and
L^{1}_{g,v}
is the same linear map on
B^{a}
and
\mathbb{R}^{a}
.
Here
E^{\prime\prime}_{v}
is the fiber of
E^{\prime\prime}
over
v
and
E^{\prime\prime}_{v}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}
is the fiber of
E^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a}
over
(v,pt)\in V\times B^{a}
.

Given a stabilization
(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime},G)
of
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},G)
and a section
s:V\to E
, we construct the
stabilization
s^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
of
s
as follows:
Let
h:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a smooth function such that
h(y)=1
on
0\leq y\leq\frac{1}{3}
and
h(y)=0
on
y\geq\frac{2}{3}
.
We then define

(5.4.3)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}(i(v,x))=(j(h(|x|)s(v)),0).}

Definition 5.4.4
.

The pair
(E^{\prime},s^{\prime})
consisting of a stabilization
E^{\prime}
of
E
and a stabilization
s^{\prime}
of
s
is called a
stabilization of
(E,s)
.

Lemma 5.4.5
.

If
(E^{\prime},s^{\prime})
is a stabilization of
(E,s)
, then
i(\overline{\partial}^{-1}_{J}(s))=(\overline{\partial}^{\prime}_{J})^{-1}(s^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the definition of
(E^{\prime},s^{\prime})
: If
i(v,x)\in(\overline{\partial}^{\prime}_{J})^{-1}(s^{\prime})
, then

\overline{\partial}^{\prime}_{J}(i(v,x))=s^{\prime}(i(v,x)),

which is equivalent to

(j(\overline{\partial}_{J}(v)),x)=(j(h(|x|)s(v)),0).

Hence
x=0
,
h(|x|)=1
, and
\overline{\partial}_{J}(v)=s(v)
. ∎

Analogously, if
{\mathfrak{s}}
is a
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse lifted multisection of
(\pi_{V}:E\to V,\overline{\partial}_{J},G)
, then we can construct the
stabilization
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathfrak{s}}
which is a lifted multisection of the stabilization
(\pi_{V^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\to V^{\prime},\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime},G)
such that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
is
\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime}
-transverse and
i(\overline{\partial}^{-1}_{J}({\mathfrak{s}}))=(\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\prime})^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})
as weighted branched manifolds. This is straightforward since the orbibundles are global quotient orbibundles.

5.4.2.
Changing
\varepsilon

The effect of changing
\varepsilon>0
can be summarized as follows:

Remark 5.4.6

(Changing
\varepsilon>0
)
.

There exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
\ell_{0}>0
such that Theorem
5.1.2
holds for all
\varepsilon>0
satisfying
\varepsilon_{0}\geq\varepsilon
and
\ell
satisfying
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
. If we fix
\ell
, then for any two
0<\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}<\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists a
1
-parameter family of
\overline{\partial}_{J}
-transverse bundles
E^{\ell,\varepsilon_{t}}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
,
t\in[1,2]
. In the process of extracting weighted branched manifolds in later sections, it follows that the weighted branched manifolds corresponding to
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
are cobordant.

6.
Gluing

The goal of this section is to define the terms that appear in the gluing theorems and also state the gluing theorems that we need.

Suppose we are considering
\operatorname{(Symp)}
case; in this case we need to quotient by the
\mathbb{R}
-translations in the target. A key observation is that
E_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
is equivariant under
\mathbb{R}
-translations by the construction in Theorem
5.1.2
. The necessary modifications can be made for the
\operatorname{(Cob)}
case.

6.1.
The setup

Let

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=k}_{J}(\dot{F},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}).

Convention 6.1.1
.

We use the notation
\overline{\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}}
to mean the space of SFT buildings in
\mathbb{R}\times M
of total Fredholm index
\operatorname{ind}=k
which limit to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}
at the positive end and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}
at the negative end, and whose domain, when glued up, has topological type
\dot{F}
. We also write

\partial(\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R})=\overline{\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}}-\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}.

Note that
\partial(\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R})
is not necessarily the set-theoretic boundary of
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}
: for example it is possible that
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}=\varnothing
and
\partial(\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R})\not=\varnothing
.

We will explain a slightly simplified situation; the general case is only notationally more involved.

Assumption 6.1.2
.

There exist

\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=k_{1}}_{J}(\dot{F}_{1},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma};\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}),

\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{ind}=k_{2}}_{J}(\dot{F}_{2},\mathbb{R}\times M;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+};\boldsymbol{\gamma}),

with
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
such that
\dot{F}_{1}
and
\dot{F}_{2}
are connected and

\partial(\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R})\supset\mathcal{M}_{1}/\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}/\mathbb{R},

where
\mathcal{M}_{2}/\mathbb{R}
has higher SFT level (i.e., has larger
s
coordinate) than
\mathcal{M}_{1}/\mathbb{R}
.
Also we choose compact subsets
K_{1}\subset\mathcal{M}_{1}/\mathbb{R}
and
K_{2}\subset\mathcal{M}_{2}/\mathbb{R}
.

For
i=1,2
,
(F_{i},{\bf p}_{i},{\bf q}_{i})
and hence
\ddot{F}_{i}
are fixed. Let
\varepsilon_{i}^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}(K_{i})>0
be the constant that appears in the definition of
s^{\prime}_{+}(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})
in Section
4.2.5
, in case
\chi(\dot{F})=0
or
1
. We take a Teichmüller slice
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
such that each
[\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i}]\in K_{i}
admits a representative
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})=((F_{i},j_{i},{\bf p}_{i},{\bf q}_{i},{\bf r}_{i}),u_{i})
with
j_{i}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
. Define

\overline{\partial}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}_{J}:\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}\to\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}}}

as before. Let
Z_{i}=(\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}})^{-1}(0)
, view
K_{i}
as a compact subset of
Z_{i}
, and let
\mathcal{N}(K_{i})
be a sufficiently small neighborhood of
K_{i}
in
\mathcal{B}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{i}}}
.

We are also assuming that

(6.1.1)

{\bf p}=({\bf p}_{1}\cup{\bf p}_{2})-({\bf p}_{+,1}\cup{\bf p}_{-,2}),\quad{\bf r}=({\bf r}_{1}\cup{\bf r}_{2})-({\bf r}_{+,1}\cup{\bf r}_{-,2}),

and
F=F_{1}\#F_{2}
, where
\#
is a multiple connected sum and the gluing occurs along subsets
{\bf p}_{+,1}\subset{\bf p}_{1}
,
{\bf p}_{-,2}\subset{\bf p}_{2}
that correspond to
{\bf r}_{+,1}\subset{\bf r}_{1}
,
{\bf r}_{-,2}\subset{\bf r}_{2}
.

We apply Theorem
5.1.2
and Section
5.2.1
to
K_{i}\subset Z_{i}
to obtain

E_{i}=E_{i}^{\ell,\varepsilon}\to V_{i}/\mathbb{R},

where
V_{i}=(\overline{\partial}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}_{J})^{-1}(E_{i})\subset\mathcal{N}(K_{i})
as in Equation (
5.2.1
) and
V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
is the quotient by the target
\mathbb{R}
-translation. By slight abuse of notation the total space of the obstruction bundle over
V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
will also be denoted by
E_{i}
. We are also assuming that the same constants
\ell,\varepsilon
apply to both
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
.

We fix a slice
V_{i}/\mathbb{R}\to V_{i}
and write
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})\in V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
to indicate the representative of its equivalence class in
V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
in that slice. We also assume that

\min_{j}\{s_{-,j}(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2})\}\gg 0,\quad\max_{j}\{s_{+,j}(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\}\ll 0

for all
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})\in V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
.

6.2.
Close to breaking

Let
\delta>0
be a small constant.
8
8

8

Unrelated to the weight
\delta
used in the Sobolev spaces earlier.
Given a constant
T\in\mathbb{R}
, let
u_{i,T}
(resp.
u_{T}
) be
u_{i}
(resp.
u
) translated up by
s=T
units.

We define the notion of
close to breaking
in the spirit of
[
HT2
, Definition 7.1]
as follows:

Definition 6.2.1

(Close to breaking)
.

A map
(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u)
is
\delta
-close to breaking into the building

(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2}),\quad(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})=((F_{i},j_{i},{\bf p}_{i},{\bf q}_{i},{\bf r}_{i}),u_{i})\in V_{i}/\mathbb{R},\quad i=1,2,

if, after possibly translating
u
, there exist
T_{2}>T_{2}^{\prime}>0
and
T_{1}>T_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that the following hold:

•

The domain
\dot{F}_{2}^{\prime}
of
u_{2,T_{2}}|_{\{s\geq T_{2}^{\prime}\}}
is obtained from
\dot{F}_{2}=F_{2}-{\bf p}_{2}
by removing ends
C_{2,\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. There is a biholomorphism

(C_{2,\gamma},j_{2})\simeq((-\infty,T_{2}^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z},j_{\operatorname{std}})

with coordinates
(s,t)
and standard complex structure
j_{\operatorname{std}}:\partial_{s}\mapsto\partial_{t}
, such that the asymptotic marker corresponds to
t=0
,
u_{2,T_{2}}|_{C_{2,\gamma}}
is
\delta
-close in the
C^{1}
-norm to the map

C_{2,\gamma}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s})\mathbb{Z}\times D\subset\mathbb{R}\times M,\quad(s,t)\mapsto(s,t,0),

where we are using simple coordinates for
\gamma
, and the postcomposition of
u_{2,T_{2}}|_{C_{2,\gamma}}
by the projection onto
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s})\mathbb{Z}
is
(j_{2},j_{\operatorname{std}})
-holomorphic.

•

The domain
\dot{F}_{1}^{\prime}
of
u_{1,-T_{1}}|_{\{s\leq-T_{1}^{\prime}\}}
satisfies an analogous condition with ends
C_{1,\gamma}
.

•

T_{2}^{\prime}+T_{1}^{\prime}\geq 1/\delta
and the domain
\dot{F}^{\prime}
of
u|_{\dot{F}-\{-T_{1}^{\prime}<s<T_{2}^{\prime}\}}
is obtained from
\dot{F}=F-{\bf p}
by removing annuli
A_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. There is a biholomorphism

(A_{\gamma},j)\simeq([-T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z},j_{\operatorname{std}})

such that
u|_{A_{\gamma}}
is
\delta
-close in the
C^{1}
-norm to the map

A_{\gamma}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s})\mathbb{Z}\times D\subset\mathbb{R}\times M,\quad(s,t)\mapsto(s,t,0),

where we are using simple coordinates for
\gamma
, and the postcomposition of
u|_{A_{\gamma}}
by the projection onto
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s})\mathbb{Z}
is
(j,j_{\operatorname{std}})
-holomorphic.

•

{\bf q}_{1},{\bf q}_{2}
, and
{\bf q}
are disjoint from
C_{1,\gamma},C_{2,\gamma}
, and
A_{\gamma}
, respectively.

•

There is a diffeomorphism

\phi:\dot{F}=F-{\bf p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\dot{F}^{\prime}_{1}\cup(\sqcup_{\gamma}A_{\gamma})\cup\dot{F}^{\prime}_{2}

which takes
\dot{F}^{\prime}
to
\dot{F}^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup\dot{F}_{2}^{\prime}
, takes
{\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}
to
{\bf q}_{1}
and
{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}
to
{\bf q}_{2}
where
{\bf q}={\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}
, is the identity on
A_{\gamma}
, and identifies the corresponding ends. Here the right-hand side is obtained by gluing the corresponding boundary components of
A_{\gamma}
and
C_{2,\gamma}
(also
A_{\gamma}
and
C_{1,\gamma}
) via their identifications with
\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\phi_{*}j|_{\ddot{F}}
is
\delta
-close in the Teichmüller metric to
j_{1}|_{\ddot{F}^{\prime}_{1}}\cup j_{\operatorname{std}}|_{\cup_{\gamma}A_{\gamma}}\cup j_{2}|_{\ddot{F}^{\prime}_{2}}
where
\ddot{F}^{\prime}_{i}=\dot{F}^{\prime}_{i}-{\bf q}_{i}
.

•

u_{2,T_{2}}|_{\dot{F}^{\prime}_{2}}
is
\delta
-close in the
C^{1}
-norm to
u|_{\dot{F}^{\prime}_{2}}
and
u_{1,-T_{1}}|_{\dot{F}^{\prime}_{1}}
is
\delta
-close in the
C^{1}
-norm to
u|_{\dot{F}^{\prime}_{1}}
via the identification
\phi
.

We also say that
{\bf q}
is
\delta
-close to
{\bf q}_{1}\cup{\bf q}_{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},V_{2}/\mathbb{R})
be the set of equivalence classes of maps

(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u)

modulo
\mathbb{R}
-translations which have representatives that are
\delta
-close to breaking into some building
(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2})
with
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})\in V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},V_{2}/\mathbb{R})
be a slice of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},V_{2}/\mathbb{R})
whose elements are maps which are
\delta
-close to breaking.

6.3.
Removable punctures

Let us write
\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}=(F,j,{\bf p},\varnothing,{\bf r})
when
\mathcal{F}=(F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r})
.

Given
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})=((F_{i},j_{i},{\bf p}_{i},{\bf q}_{i},{\bf r}_{i}),u_{i})
,
i=1,2
, and
(\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing},u)
which is
\delta
-close to
(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\varnothing},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{\varnothing},u_{2})
, we explain how to select removable punctures
{\bf q}={\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}
for
F
following Section
4.2.5
, so that
(\mathcal{F},u)
is
\delta
-close to
(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2})
for possibly larger
\delta
: Let
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}
and
\gamma_{\pm}\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\pm}
. Denote the ends and the neck regions of
u
by
u(s,t)=(s,t,\eta(s,t))
using simple coordinates such that
s\in[T_{2}^{\prime\prime},\infty)
,
s\in(-\infty,-T_{1}^{\prime\prime}]
, and
s\in[-T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}]
. If
\chi(\dot{F}_{i})<0
, then
{\bf q}_{i}^{\prime}=\varnothing
, where
i=1,2
. If
\chi(\dot{F}_{1})\geq 0
, then as in Lemma
4.2.8
we define

(6.3.1)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u)

\displaystyle:=\max\{s\in[-T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}]~|~I_{\gamma}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)-\varepsilon^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\gamma_{-},-}(\mathcal{F},u)

\displaystyle:=\min\{s\in(-\infty,-T_{1}^{\prime\prime}]~|~I_{\gamma_{-}}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{-})+\varepsilon^{\prime}\}.

If
\chi(\dot{F}_{2})\geq 0
, then

(6.3.2)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\gamma_{+},+}(\mathcal{F},u)

\displaystyle:=\max\{s\in[T_{2}^{\prime\prime},\infty)~|~I_{\gamma_{+}}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{+})-\varepsilon^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\gamma,-}(\mathcal{F},u)

\displaystyle:=\min\{s\in[-T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}]~|~I_{\gamma}((\mathcal{F},u),s^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma)+\varepsilon^{\prime}\}.

Then
{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}
(resp.
{\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}
) is a list of punctures defined using
s^{\prime}_{\gamma_{+},+}(\mathcal{F},u)
and
s^{\prime}_{\gamma,-}(\mathcal{F},u)
(resp.
s^{\prime}_{\gamma,+}(\mathcal{F},u)
and
s^{\prime}_{\gamma_{-},-}(\mathcal{F},u)
)) and
(Q)
in Section
4.2.4
. Hence for
(\mathcal{F},u)
close to breaking into
(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2})
, the data
(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup(\mathcal{F}_{2},u_{2})
and
(\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing},u)
are sufficient to recover
{\bf q}
, i.e., there exists a
puncturing map

(6.3.3)

\mathfrak{Punc}:(\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing},u)\mapsto(\mathcal{F},u)

such that
{\bf q}
satisfies Equations (
6.3.1
) and (
6.3.2
).

6.4.
The bundle
E^{\prime}

We define the bundle

(6.4.1)

E^{\prime}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},V_{2}/\mathbb{R})

for
\delta>0
small as follows: Given
(\mathcal{F},u)=((F,j,{\bf p},{\bf q},{\bf r}),u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},V_{2}/\mathbb{R})
, consider the
\varepsilon
-thin part
\operatorname{Thin}_{\varepsilon}(\ddot{F},g)
for
g=g_{(\ddot{F},j)}
as in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
: it is a union of cusps (as in Equation (
5.1.2
)) and annuli.

Let
C
be a boundary component of
\operatorname{Thin}_{\varepsilon}(\ddot{F},g)
.
We then apply Definition
5.1.5
to obtain the average
s_{C}(\mathcal{F},u)
of the
s
-coordinates of
u|_{C}
over

t=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s}),2\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s}),\dots,m(\gamma)\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma^{s}),

where
\gamma
is the orbit corresponding to
C
,
\gamma^{s}
is the simple orbit underlying
\gamma
, and
m(\gamma)
is the multiplicity of
\gamma
over
\gamma^{s}
. Note that
s_{C}(\mathcal{F},u)
agrees with
s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)
from the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
when
C
is the boundary of the cusp corresponding to the
i
th
\pm
end of
(\mathcal{F},u)
. We then define
E^{\ell,\varepsilon}_{(\mathcal{F},u),C}
as in Equation (
5.1.4
), using
s_{C}(\mathcal{F},u)
.
We then set

(6.4.2)

E^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}:=\bigoplus_{C}E^{\ell,\varepsilon}_{(\mathcal{F},u),C},

where
C
ranges over all boundary components
C
of
\operatorname{Thin}_{\varepsilon}(\ddot{F},g)
corresponding to cusps or glued edges.

6.5.
Statements of gluing theorems

In this subsection we collect the gluing theorems that we need to construct the semi-global Kuranishi structures. They are standard gluing results and will be proven in the next section.

In general, the
2
-component building condition in Assumption
6.1.2
is replaced by an analogous
m
-component condition: We have moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, such that

\partial(\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R})\supset\mathcal{M}_{1}/\mathbb{R}\times\dots\times\mathcal{M}_{m}/\mathbb{R},

and compact subsets
K_{i}
of
\mathcal{M}_{i}/\mathbb{R}
. Let
Z_{i}=(\overline{\partial}_{J}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}})^{-1}(0)
, view
K_{i}
as a compact subset of
Z_{i}
, and let
E_{i}|_{V_{i}}\to V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
be the bundle from Section
5.2.1
with
V_{i}
a small neighborhood of
K_{i}
. We are gluing
m
non-trivial (= not trivial cylinder) components, one from each
V_{i}
, with a prescribed identification of a subset of ends. The notion of a map
(\mathcal{F},u)
that is
\delta
-close to breaking into the building

(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup\dots\cup(\mathcal{F}_{m},u_{m})

with
(\mathcal{F}_{i},u_{i})\in V_{i}/\mathbb{R}
is defined as in the
2
-component case. Hence we can define
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R})
as the set of equivalence classes of maps with representatives that are
\delta
-close to
(\mathcal{F}_{1},u_{1})\cup\dots\cup(\mathcal{F}_{m},u_{m})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R})
as a slice of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R})
. Then we can define

(6.5.1)

E^{\prime}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R}),

using Equation (
6.4.2
) (the definitions of
s_{C}(\mathcal{F},u)
and
E^{\ell,\varepsilon}_{(\mathcal{F},u),C}
carry over with no change) and

(6.5.2)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R}):=\overline{\partial}_{J}^{-1}(E^{\prime})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R}).

We also introduce the notation
V_{i}^{\text{sh}}
(resp.
V_{i}^{\text{en}}
) to denote a slight
\mathbb{R}
-invariant shrinking (resp. enlargement) of
V_{i}
which contains
K_{i}
and is still open.

Theorem 6.5.1

(Gluing)
.

(A)

For a sufficiently large gluing parameter bound
R=R(K_{1},\dots,K_{m})>0
, there exists a gluing map

(6.5.3)

G_{(1,\dots,m)}:(V_{1}/\mathbb{R})\times\dots\times(V_{m}/\mathbb{R})\times[R,\infty)^{m-1}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R})

which is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image and whose image contains

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}}_{\delta^{\prime}}(V_{1}^{\text{sh}}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}^{\text{sh}}/\mathbb{R})

for sufficiently small
\delta^{\prime}>0
.

(B)

The gluing map
G_{(1,\dots,m)}
does not depend on the choices of
K_{1},\dots,K_{m}
.
More precisely, for another choice of compact subsets
K_{i}^{\prime}\supset K_{i}
and neighborhoods
V^{\prime}_{i}\supset K^{\prime}_{i}
such that
V^{\prime}_{i}\supset V_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, there exist a gluing parameter bound
R^{\prime}=R(K^{\prime}_{1},\dots,K^{\prime}_{m})>R=R(K_{1},\dots,K_{m})>0
and a gluing map

G^{\prime}_{(1,\dots,m)}:(V^{\prime}_{1}/\mathbb{R})\times\dots\times(V^{\prime}_{m}/\mathbb{R})\times[R^{\prime},\infty)^{m-1}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}}_{\delta}(V^{\prime}_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V^{\prime}_{m}/\mathbb{R}).

If we use the same
\ell,\varepsilon
, then
G^{\prime}_{(1,\dots,m)}=G_{(1,\dots,m)}
on their common domain

(V_{1}/\mathbb{R})\times\dots\times(V_{m}/\mathbb{R})\times[R^{\prime},\infty)^{m-1}.

Remark 6.5.2
.

In Theorem
6.5.1
(B), it is important that we use the same
\ell,\varepsilon
to define
V_{i}
and
V_{i}^{\prime}
. The use of the orthogonal projection (Equation (
7.4.19
)) in Equation (
7.5.1
), which depends on
\ell,\varepsilon
, is the source of slight discrepancies when applying the contraction mapping theorem.

Assuming that for
1\leq i<j\leq m
,
V_{i},\dots,V_{j}
can be glued into a one-component curve and writing
V_{(i,\dots,j)}/\mathbb{R}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}_{(i,\dots,j)}}_{\delta^{\prime}}(V_{i}^{\text{sh}}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{j}^{\text{sh}}/\mathbb{R})
, where
E^{\prime}_{(i,\dots,j)}
is
E^{\prime}
for
V_{i}/\mathbb{R},\dots
,
V_{j}/\mathbb{R}
, we also have the following:

Theorem 6.5.3

(Iterated gluing)
.

For a sufficiently large gluing parameter bound
R=R(K_{1},\dots,K_{m})>0
, there exists a gluing map

\displaystyle G_{(1,\dots,(i,\dots,j),\dots m)}:(V_{1}/\mathbb{R})\times\dots\times(V_{i-1}/\mathbb{R})\times(V_{(i,\dots,j)}/\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\times(V_{j+1}/\mathbb{R})\times\dots\times(V_{m}/\mathbb{R})\times[R,\infty)^{m+i-j-1}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{E^{\prime}_{(1,\dots,m)}}_{\delta}(V_{1}/\mathbb{R},\dots,V_{m}/\mathbb{R}),

such that
G_{(1,\dots,(i,\dots,j),\dots m)}\circ(\operatorname{id},\dots,G_{(i,\dots,j)},\dots,\operatorname{id})
is
C^{1}
-close to the gluing map
G_{(1,\dots,m)}

and the error goes to
0
as
\min_{i=1}^{m-1}(T_{i})\to\infty
, where
(T_{1},\dots,T_{m-1})\in[R,\infty)^{m-1}
. An analogous statement also holds for higher-order iterated gluing maps.

7.
Details of gluing

We prove Theorem
6.5.1
with
m=2
, following the general outline of
[
HT2
]
and
[
BH
]
. The case for general
m
only differs in notation. The proof of Theorem
6.5.3
also uses the same type of estimates as in Theorem
6.5.1
and we only provide a sketch.

We are assuming that
\varepsilon>0
is small, that
(\alpha,J)
is an
L
-simple pair (see Definition
3.1.4
), and that
J
is smooth.

We write
E_{+}\to V_{+}
for
E_{2}\to V_{2}
and
E_{-}\to V_{-}
for
E_{1}\to V_{1}
. Let
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})_{\mathbb{R}}\in V_{\pm}/\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}=(F_{\pm},j_{\pm},{\bf p}_{\pm},{\bf r}_{\pm})
. For simplicity we assume that
(\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})
is a curve from
\gamma_{+}
to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})
and
(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})
is a curve from
\gamma_{1}
to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}=(\gamma_{-,1},\dots,\gamma_{-,l})
, i.e., we are gluing the first negative end of
(\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})
to the positive end of
(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})
. We fix representatives
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})
of
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
s_{\mp,1}(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})=0
. Here
s_{\pm,i}(\mathcal{F},u)
is as defined in the proof of Theorem
5.1.2
. We write “
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})\in V_{\pm}/\mathbb{R}
” to mean
(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})
is the chosen representative in its equivalence class. Note that the elements of
(E_{\pm})_{(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm})}
are supported on
-1\leq s\leq 0
for
(\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})
and on
0\leq s\leq 1
for
(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})
.

Notation 7.0.1
.

In general, if we decorate
(\mathcal{F},u)
with subscripts and superscripts as in
(\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\star},u^{*}_{\star})
, then
\mathcal{F}^{*}_{\star}=(F^{*}_{\star},j^{*}_{\star},{\bf p}^{*}_{\star},{\bf r}^{*}_{\star})
and
\dot{F}^{*}_{\star}=F^{*}_{\star}-{\bf p}^{*}_{\star}
.

In the next several subsections we retrace the steps of Sections 8.1–8.4 of
[
BH
]
, adapted to the current setting.

7.1.
Pregluing

Fix a constant
T_{0}\gg 0
. Also let
T\gg T_{0}
, which is allowed to vary.

Notation 7.1.1
.

If
\tau_{T}:\mathbb{R}\times M\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}\times M
is given by the translation
(s,x)\mapsto(s+T,x)
, then let
u_{\pm,T}=\tau_{\pm(T+T_{0})}\circ u_{\pm}
. In general a subscript or superscript
+,T
(resp.
-,T
) indicates the result of translating up (resp. down) by
s=T+T_{0}
.

Recall the simple coordinates
(s,t,x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}))
on a sufficiently small neighborhood
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{1})\mathbb{Z}\times D
of
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma_{1}
; here we are slightly abusing notation and passing to a finite cover if
\gamma_{1}
is not simple. We assume that
\varepsilon>0
which appears in the definition of
E^{\ell,\varepsilon}
is sufficiently small so that
u_{-}|_{s\geq-T_{0}}
(resp.
u_{+}|_{s\leq T_{0}}
) can be written as a graph
(s,t,\eta_{-}(s,t))
(resp.
(s,t,\eta_{+}(s,t))
) over
[0,\infty)\times\gamma_{1}
(resp.
(-\infty,0]\times\gamma_{1}
).

Fix constants
0<h<1
and
r\gg h^{-1}
. We take
T_{0}>5r
.
Choose a cutoff function
\beta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that
\beta(s)=0
for
s\leq 0
and
\beta(s)=1
for
s\geq 1
. Let
\beta_{-,T}(s)=\beta(\frac{T-s}{hr})
and
\beta_{+,T}(s)=\beta(\frac{T+s}{hr})
.

For
T^{\prime}\leq T+T_{0}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{+,T^{\prime}}
be the component of

u_{+,T}^{-1}(\{s<T^{\prime}\})\simeq u_{+}^{-1}(\{s<T^{\prime}-(T+T_{0})\})

corresponding to
\gamma_{1}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{-,T^{\prime}}
be

u_{-,T}^{-1}(\{s>-T^{\prime}\})\simeq u_{-}^{-1}(\{s>-T^{\prime}+(T+T_{0})\}),

and let
A_{[T_{1},T_{2}]}
be the annulus
[T_{1},T_{2}]\times\mathbb{R}/\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{1})\mathbb{Z}
with coordinates
(s,t)
and the standard complex structure.

Figure 1.
The cutoff functions
\beta_{\pm,T}
. The little bump functions indicate the support of
(E_{\pm})_{(\mathcal{F}_{\pm},u_{\pm,T})}

Definition 7.1.2

(Pregluing)
.

(1)

The
pregluing
u_{*}=u_{*}((\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})_{\mathbb{R}},(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})_{\mathbb{R}},T)
of
(\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}\in V_{+}/\mathbb{R}
and
(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})_{\mathbb{R}}\in V_{-}/\mathbb{R}
with gluing parameter
T
is defined as follows:
9
9

9

In the case of gluing
(\mathcal{F}_{+},u_{+})_{\mathbb{R}}
and
(\mathcal{F}_{-},u_{-})_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have made a specific choice of
u^{*}
