) comes from the fact that for the Case (ii) gluing, translating a representative of an element in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathscr{K}(\gamma_{+},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{+}^{\prime}))
along the positive
\mathbb{R}
-direction moves the glued curve towards boundary; for the Case (iii) gluing, translating a representative of an element in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathscr{K}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-}))
along the positive
\mathbb{R}
-direction moves the glued curve away from the boundary. ∎

9.4.2.
Proof of invariance

For
(i,j)=(1,2),(i,j)=(2,3)
, or
(i,j)=(1,3)
, let
(\widehat{W}_{ij},\widehat{\alpha}_{ij})
be the completion of a Liouville cobordism
(W_{ij},\alpha_{ij})
from
(M_{i},\alpha_{i})
to
(M_{j},\alpha_{j})
such that
(W_{12},\alpha_{12},J_{12})
and
(W_{23},\alpha_{23},J_{23})
can be glued to give
(W_{13},\alpha_{13},J_{13})
and let

\mathcal{C}_{ij}=(M_{i},M_{j},L_{i},L_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},J_{i},J_{j},\mathscr{K}^{L_{i}},\mathscr{K}^{L_{j}},\mathfrak{S}_{i},\mathfrak{S}_{j},\mathcal{S}^{L_{i}}_{\alpha_{i}},\mathcal{S}^{L_{j}}_{\alpha_{j}},\widehat{W}_{ij},\widehat{\alpha}_{ij},J_{ij},\mathscr{K}^{(L_{+},L_{-})}_{ij},\mathfrak{S}_{ij})

be a collection of data as in Section
9.2
for
(\widehat{W}_{ij},\widehat{\alpha}_{ij})
. Using the data
\mathcal{C}_{ij}
we obtain a chain map

\Phi_{ij}:\mathfrak{A}^{L_{i}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(M_{i};\mathbb{Z})]}\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(W_{ij};\mathbb{Z})]\to\mathfrak{A}^{L_{j}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(M_{j};\mathbb{Z})]}\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(W_{ij};\mathbb{Z})].

Proposition 9.4.2

(Composition of chain maps)
.

There exists a degree 1 map

\Omega_{K}:\mathfrak{A}^{L_{1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(M_{1};\mathbb{Z})]}\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(W_{13};\mathbb{Z})]\to\mathfrak{A}^{L_{3}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(M_{3};\mathbb{Z})]}\mathbb{Q}[H_{2}(W_{13};\mathbb{Z})]

such that, after the appropriate base changes (but keeping the same notation),

\Phi_{13}-\Phi_{23}\Phi_{12}=\partial\circ\Omega_{K}-\Omega_{K}\circ\partial.

In particular,
\Phi_{23*}\Phi_{12*}=\Phi_{13*}.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as that of Proposition
9.1.4
.
∎

Finally we are ready to prove the following:

Theorem 9.4.3
.

The contact homology algebra
HC(\mathcal{D})
is independent of the choices
\mathcal{D}
and hence is an invariant of
(M,\xi)
.

Proof.

Given two choices of data
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, there exist two direct limits

\displaystyle HC(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\longrightarrow}HC^{L_{i}}(\alpha_{i},J_{i},\mathscr{K}^{L_{i}}(\alpha_{i},J_{i}),\mathfrak{S}_{i}),

\displaystyle HC(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\longrightarrow}HC^{L^{\prime}_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i},\mathscr{K}^{L^{\prime}_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i}),\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\prime}),

where the maps for
\mathcal{D}
are written as
\Phi_{i*}
and the maps for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are written as
\Phi_{i*}^{\prime}
.
We abbreviate:

\displaystyle HC^{L_{i}}

\displaystyle:=HC^{L_{i}}(\alpha_{i},J_{i},\mathscr{K}^{L_{i}}(\alpha_{i},J_{i}),\mathfrak{S}_{i}),

\displaystyle HC^{L^{\prime}_{i}}

\displaystyle:=HC^{L^{\prime}_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i},\mathscr{K}^{L^{\prime}_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i},J^{\prime}_{i}),\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\prime}).

For each
i
, let
j(i)
be the smallest
j
such that
L_{i}<L^{\prime}_{j}
.
Given a Liouville cobordism from
(M,\alpha_{i},J_{i})
to
(M,\alpha^{\prime}_{j(i)},J^{\prime}_{j(i)})
with Kuranishi data
\mathscr{K}^{L_{i}}(\alpha_{i},J_{i}),\mathfrak{S}_{i}
and
\mathscr{K}^{L_{j(i)}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{j(i)}^{\prime},J_{j(i)}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}_{j(i)}
, one can define a map
\Psi_{i*}:HC^{L_{i}}\to HC^{L^{\prime}_{j(i)}}
which satisfies

\Psi_{i+1*}\Phi_{i*}=\Phi_{j(i+1)-1*}^{\prime}\dots\Phi_{j(i)+1*}^{\prime}\Phi_{j(i)*}^{\prime}\Psi_{i*}.

Similarly, we obtain maps
\Theta_{j*}:HC^{L^{\prime}_{j}}\to HC^{L_{i(j)}}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfy

\Theta_{j+1*}\Phi^{\prime}_{j*}=\Phi_{i(j+1)-1*}\dots\Phi_{i(j)+1*}\Phi_{i(j)*}\Theta_{j*}.

Hence we obtain two direct limit maps

\Psi_{*}:HC(\mathcal{D})\to HC(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}),\quad\Theta_{*}:HC(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\to HC(\mathcal{D}).

Note that, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the two maps

\Theta_{j(k)*}\Psi_{k*}\text{ and }\Phi_{i(j(k))-1*}\dots\Phi_{k+1*}\Phi_{k*}:HC^{L_{k}}\to HC^{L_{i(j(k))}}

are the same. Therefore
\Theta_{*}\Psi_{*}=\operatorname{id}
; similarly
\Psi_{*}\Theta_{*}=\operatorname{id}
.
∎

Appendix

In the Appendix we prove Lemma
4.2.7
and Lemma
5.1.7
. We first start with some basic facts about Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 9.4.4
.

Suppose that
p=2
and
k\geq 3
. If
g,h\in W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then
g\cdot h\in W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proof.

We prove that
(g\cdot h)^{(m)}\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
0\leq m\leq k
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a domain with smooth boundary and compact closure. For
m=0
, we have
g,h\in C^{0}(\Omega)
20
20

20

More precisely,
g|_{\Omega},h|_{\Omega}
are in
C^{0}(\Omega)
; by abuse of notation we often write
g,h
for
g|_{\Omega},h|_{\Omega}
.
by the Sobolev embedding theorem, which states that
\|f\|_{C^{r}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}
for some constant
C_{\Omega}
if
k-\frac{n}{p}=k-\frac{2}{p}>r
. Hence
g\cdot h\in C^{0}(\Omega)
and
g\cdot h\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. For
m>0
, we have:

\|(g\cdot h)^{(m)}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{m}{i}\|g^{(i)}\cdot h^{(m-i)}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

Observe that one of
i
or
m-i
is
\leq\frac{m}{2}\leq\frac{k}{2}
. This implies that
g^{(i)}\in C^{0}(\Omega)
or
h^{(m-i)}\in C^{0}(\Omega)
since
g,h\in C^{k-1}(\Omega)
by the Sobolev embedding theorem. Hence
\|(g\cdot h)^{(m)}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}<\infty.

∎

Lemma 9.4.5
.

Suppose that
p=2
and
k\geq 3
. If
G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
and
H\in W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
then
G\circ H\in W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

By the Sobolev embedding theorem,
H\in C^{k-1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
\Omega
is as before, so
G(H)=G\circ H\in C^{k-1}(\Omega)\subset W^{k-1,p}(\Omega)
. This in particular implies that
G(H)\in L^{p}(\Omega)
. By the same argument, we also have
G^{\prime}(H)\in W^{k-1,p}(\Omega)
. Hence
(G(H))^{\prime}=G^{\prime}(H)\cdot H^{\prime}\in W^{k-1,p}(\Omega)
by Lemma
9.4.4
. The lemma then follows.
∎

We assume that
p=2
and
k\geq 4
(see Remark
4.2.3
). We focus on a positive puncture
p
of
\dot{F}
around which the maps that we are interested in converge to
\gamma_{+}
. Let
\dot{D}\subset\dot{F}
be an open disk about
p
and let
(\sigma,\tau)
be smooth coordinates on
\dot{D}
. For any
u
of the class
W^{k+1,p}
that is sufficiently close to a
J
-holomorphic curve, there is a map

\phi_{u}:\dot{D}\to\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}

defined by

(\sigma,\tau)\mapsto(s,t)=(s\circ u(\sigma,\tau),t\circ u(\sigma,\tau)),

where
(\sigma,\tau)
is a cylindrical holomorphic coordinate of
\dot{D}
,

s\circ u
is the
s
-coordinate of
u
, and
t\circ u
is the
t
-coordinate of
u
. Then
(s,t)
, viewed as a function of
(\sigma,\tau)
, is of class
W^{k+1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}.

Proof of Lemma
4.2.7
.

It suffices to show that
I(\cdot,s_{0})
is
C^{1}
for each
s_{0}>T^{\prime}
.

Given
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
and
\xi\in W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T(\mathbb{R}\times M))
, let
\{u^{\ell}\}_{-\epsilon<\ell<\epsilon}
be a smooth path in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{(\mathcal{F}_{0},u_{0})}
with
u^{0}=u
and
\frac{d}{d\ell}\big|_{\ell=0}u^{\ell}=\xi
. Let us decompose
\xi=(\xi^{s,t},\xi^{x,y})
into the
(s,t)
- and
(x,y)
-components and write
\eta^{\ell}=\eta+\ell\xi^{x,y}
for the
(x,y)
-component of
u^{\ell}
. Then

\displaystyle dI((\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle u),s_{0})(\xi,0)=\tfrac{d}{d\ell}\big|_{\ell=0}I((\mathcal{F},u^{\ell}),s_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\mathcal{A}(\gamma_{+})}\tfrac{d}{d\ell}\big|_{\ell=0}\alpha(t,(\eta^{\ell}\circ\phi^{-1}_{u^{\ell}})(s_{0},t))(\partial_{t}+d(\eta^{\ell}\circ\phi^{-1}_{u^{\ell}})(s_{0},t)(\partial_{t}))dt.

Recalling that

\alpha=(c(\gamma)+Q(x,y))dt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}dy_{i}-y_{i}dx_{i})

near
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma_{+}
, where
Q
is quadratic in
x
and
y
, we see that the terms in the integrand (after the term
\tfrac{d}{d\ell}\big|_{\ell=0}
) are quadratic in
\eta^{\ell}\circ\phi^{-1}_{u^{\ell}}
or bilinear in
(\eta^{\ell}\circ\phi^{-1}_{u^{\ell}},\nabla(\eta^{\ell}\circ\phi^{-1}_{u^{\ell}}))
. Since we can use the Sobolev inequality,
C^{0}
-bounds in the integrand are sufficient and we obtain

\displaystyle|dI((\mathcal{F},u),s_{0})(\xi,0)|\leq\varphi(\|u\|_{W^{k+1,2}})\cdot\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,2}},

where
k\geq 4
(i.e., we are allowed two derivatives) and
\varphi(\|u\|_{W^{k+1,2}})
is a continuous function that depends on
\|u\|_{W^{k+1,2}}
.
Hence
I
is differentiable. Similarly, we can show that
I
is
C^{1}
. ∎

In order to prove Lemma
5.1.7
, we first treat the case
\chi(\dot{F})<0
. To simplify the notation in Equation (
5.1.3
), we write
\widetilde{f}(s,t)
for
\widetilde{f}^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{j}(s,t)
,
\rho_{s_{+}}(s)
for
\frac{\partial\beta^{\pm}_{s_{\pm,i}}}{\partial s}(s)
(recall
\rho_{s_{+}}(s)
has compact support), and
f(t)
for
f^{\gamma_{\pm,i}}_{j}(t)
.
With respect to the coordinates
s,t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
,
Equation (
5.1.3
) can be written as

(9.4.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(s,t):=\rho_{s_{+}}(s)f(t)\otimes\pi_{j}(ds-idt).

First observe that
\rho_{s_{+}}(s)
and
f(t)
are smooth functions of
(s,t)
, so viewed as functions of
(\sigma,\tau)
, they are in class
W^{k+1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
by Lemma
9.4.5
. Next
the projection
\pi_{j}
is smooth, so
\pi_{j}(ds-idt)\in W_{\operatorname{loc}}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}T^{*}\dot{F})
. Hence
\widetilde{f}\in W^{k+1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}u^{*}T\widehat{W})
by Lemma
9.4.4
, and this proves the first part of Lemma
5.1.7
, namely
E^{\ell,\varepsilon}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}\subset\mathcal{E}_{(\mathcal{F},u)}
for all
(\mathcal{F},u)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
.

For
(j,v)\in\mathcal{N}(K)
with
v:\dot{F}\to\widehat{W}
smooth, there exists a small neighborhood
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{W}
of
(j,v)
inside
\mathcal{N}(K)
with
\mathcal{V}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{W}\subset\{\exp(v,\xi)~|~\xi\in W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},v^{*}T\widehat{W})\}
.
We can trivialize
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{D}}
by identifying

\mathcal{E}_{(j^{\prime},u)}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}_{j^{\prime}}u^{*}T\widehat{W})

with

\mathcal{E}_{(j,v)}=W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}_{j}v^{*}T\widehat{W})

for any
(j^{\prime},u)\in\mathcal{D}
via the exponential map. More precisely, we define

\Psi:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}_{j^{\prime}}u^{*}T\widehat{W})\to W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}_{j}v^{*}T\widehat{W})

\zeta=\eta\otimes\alpha\mapsto\operatorname{Par}_{\xi}\eta\otimes\pi_{j}\alpha,

where
\xi=(\exp_{v})^{-1}u
,
\eta\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})
,
\alpha\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}T^{*}F)
,

\operatorname{Par}_{\xi}:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},u^{*}T\widehat{W})\to W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},v^{*}T\widehat{W})

is the parallel transport along the path
\{\exp(v,(1-t)\xi)\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}
, and

\pi_{j}:W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{1}T^{*}F)\to W^{k,p}_{\delta}(\dot{F},\wedge^{0,1}_{j}T^{*}F)

is the projection with respect to
g_{(\dot{F},j)}
.

Let
\widetilde{f}
be the section of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
with

\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(s,t)=\rho_{s_{+}}(s)f(t)\otimes\pi_{j^{\prime}}(ds-idt),

where
(s,t)
are viewed as coordinates of
\dot{F}
via
\phi_{u}
as usual. We show that
\widetilde{f}
is a
C^{1}
-section of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
. In the next several paragraphs we calculate the derivative
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)
.

We first calculate
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)
, where
\{u_{l_{1}}\}_{-\epsilon\leq l_{1}\leq\epsilon}
is a smooth path in
\mathcal{W}
with
u_{0}=u
and
\xi=\frac{d}{dl_{1}}|_{l_{1}=0}u_{l_{1}}
:

(9.4.5)

\displaystyle d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)=

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}|_{l_{1}=0}\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u_{l_{1}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(9.4.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}\rho_{s_{+}(l_{1})}(s_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}f(t)\otimes\pi_{j^{\prime}}(ds-idt)

(9.4.7)

\displaystyle+\rho_{s_{+}(0)}(s)\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}f(t_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}\otimes\pi_{j^{\prime}}(ds-idt)

(9.4.8)

\displaystyle+\rho_{s_{+}(0)}(s)f(t)\otimes\pi_{j^{\prime}}\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}(ds_{l_{1}}-idt_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0},

where
(s_{l_{1}},t_{l_{1}})=\phi_{u_{l_{1}}}(\sigma,\tau)
.

Until the end of the Appendix,
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
means
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
with domain
\dot{D}
. We claim that
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}
. Note that the weight
\delta
can be ignored since
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)
has compact support. Since
\xi\in W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}
,

•

\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}s_{l_{1}}|_{l_{1}=0},\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}t_{l_{1}}|_{l_{1}=0}
,
\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}f(t_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}\in W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}
; and

•

\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}(ds_{l_{1}}-idt_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}
.

Next we consider the term
\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}\rho_{s_{+}(l_{1})}(s_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}
. If we write
\rho_{s_{+}}(s)
as
\rho(s_{+},s)
, then
\rho(\cdot,\cdot)
is smooth in both variables. Observe that

\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}\rho(s_{+}(l_{1}),s_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}=\tfrac{\partial\rho}{\partial s_{+}}(s_{+}(0),s)\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}s_{+}(l_{1})|_{l_{1}=0}+\tfrac{\partial\rho}{\partial s}(s_{+}(0),s)\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}s_{l_{1}}|_{l_{1}=0}.

Lemma 9.4.6
.

s_{+}:\mathcal{N}(K)\to\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{1}
-map.

The proof of this lemma will be postponed until the very end. By Lemma
9.4.6
,
\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}\rho(s_{+}(l_{1}),s_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}\in W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}
. It follows that
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}
.

Next we calculate
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(\mathfrak{j},0)
with
\mathfrak{j}\in T_{j^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
\{j_{l_{2}}\}_{-\epsilon\leq l_{2}\leq\epsilon}
be a smooth path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
with
j_{0}=j^{\prime}
and
\frac{d}{dl_{2}}|_{l_{2}=0}j_{l_{2}}=\mathfrak{j}
. Then

(9.4.9)

\displaystyle d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(\mathfrak{j},0)=

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}|_{l_{2}=0}\widetilde{f}(j_{l_{2}},u)

(9.4.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}\rho_{s_{+}(l_{2})}(s)|_{l_{2}=0}f(t)\otimes\pi_{j^{\prime}}(ds-idt)

(9.4.11)

\displaystyle+\rho_{s_{+}(0)}(s)f(t)\otimes\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}\pi_{j_{l_{2}}}(ds-idt)|_{l_{2}=0}.

Since
\pi_{j}
depends smoothly on
j
, we have
\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}\pi_{j_{l_{2}}}(ds-idt)|_{l_{2}=0}\in W^{k,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Again we write
\rho_{s_{+}}(s)
as
\rho(s_{+},s)
and obtain

\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}\rho(s_{+}(l_{2}),s)|_{l_{2}=0}=\tfrac{\partial\rho}{\partial s_{+}}(s_{+}(0),s)\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}s_{+}(l_{2})|_{l_{2}=0}.

By Lemma
9.4.6
,
\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}\rho(s_{+}(l_{2}),s)|_{l_{2}=0}\in W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}
. Hence
d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(\mathfrak{j},0)\in W^{k,p}_{\delta}
.

We now bound the
W^{k,p}_{\delta}
-norm of each term in Equations (
9.4.5
) and (
9.4.9
):

•

(
9.4.6
) is bounded above by
C\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}}\cdot C(j^{\prime})\cdot\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}}
;

•

(
9.4.7
) by
C\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}}\cdot C(j^{\prime})\cdot\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}}
;

•

(
9.4.8
) by
C\cdot C(j^{\prime})\cdot\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}}
;

•

(
9.4.10
) by
C\|\mathfrak{j}\|\cdot C(j^{\prime})\cdot\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}}
; and

•

(
9.4.11
) by
C\|\mathfrak{j}\|\cdot\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}}
.

Here
C(j^{\prime})
is a positive continuous function of
j^{\prime}
and
g
is a smooth cutoff function which equals one on
s_{+}(v)-c\leq s\leq s_{+}(v)+c
for some
c>0
and has compact support on
\dot{D}
.
Hence

\|d\widetilde{f}(j^{\prime},u)(\mathfrak{j},\xi)\|_{W^{k,p}_{\delta}}\leq C(C(j^{\prime})+C(j^{\prime})\cdot\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}}+\|gu\|_{W^{k+1,p}})(\|\mathfrak{j}\|+\|\xi\|_{W^{k+1,p}_{\delta}}),

which implies that
\widetilde{f}
is a differentiable section of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{N}(K)}\to\mathcal{N}(K)
. Almost the same calculation shows that
\widetilde{f}
is in
C^{1}
.

Proof of Lemma
9.4.6
.

Let
r_{j^{\prime}}:\dot{F}\to\mathbb{R}
be the injectivity radius map, i.e,
r_{j^{\prime}}
maps
x\in\dot{F}
to the injectivity radius
\operatorname{inj}(x)
with respect to
g_{(\dot{F},j)}
. The map
r_{j}
is smooth on the ends of
\dot{F}
and also smoothly depends on
j^{\prime}
. For any
(j^{\prime},u)\in\mathcal{D}
, consider the map

(9.4.12)

R_{(j^{\prime},u)}:[R,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad s\mapsto r_{j^{\prime}}(\phi^{-1}_{u}(s,0)),

where
R\gg 0
and depends on
(j^{\prime},u)
.
Note that
R_{(j^{\prime},u)}
is invertible near the positive end.

When
\chi(\dot{F})<0
, we have

(9.4.13)

\displaystyle s_{+}(j^{\prime},u)=R_{(j^{\prime},u)}^{-1}(\varepsilon).

Differentiating the equation

(9.4.14)

r_{j^{\prime}}(\phi^{-1}_{u_{l_{1}}}(s_{+}(j^{\prime},u_{l_{1}}),0))=\varepsilon

with respect to
l_{1}
and rearranging, we obtain:

(9.4.15)

\displaystyle ds_{+}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\xi)=\tfrac{d}{dl_{1}}s_{+}(j^{\prime},u_{l_{1}})|_{l_{1}=0}=-\frac{dr_{j^{\prime}}(z)\circ(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}(\xi^{s,t}(z))}{dr_{j^{\prime}}(z)\circ(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}(\partial_{s})},

where
\partial_{s}
is a vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times S^{1}
,
\xi^{s,t}(z)
is the
(s,t)
-component of
\xi
at
z
, and
z=\phi_{u}^{-1}(s_{+}(j^{\prime},u),0).
Here
\frac{d}{dl_{1}}\phi^{-1}_{u_{l_{1}}}(s_{+}(j^{\prime},u),0))|_{l_{1}=0}=(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}(\xi^{s,t}(z))
. Note that all terms are evaluated at the point
z
and
z
continuously depends on
(j^{\prime},u)
, so
dr_{j^{\prime}}(z)\circ(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}
and
dr_{j^{\prime}}(z)\circ(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}(\partial_{s})
depend continuously on
(j^{\prime},u)
. Hence
ds_{+}(j^{\prime},u)(0,\cdot)
is a bounded linear map from
W_{\delta}^{k+1,p}(\dot{F},v^{*}T\widehat{W})
to
\mathbb{R}
which depends continuously on
(j^{\prime},u)
.

Next, differentiating the equation

(9.4.16)

r_{j_{l_{2}}}(\phi^{-1}_{u}(s_{+}(j_{l_{2}},u),0))=\varepsilon

with respect to
l_{2}
, we obtain

(9.4.17)

\displaystyle ds_{+}(j^{\prime},u)(\mathfrak{j},0)=\tfrac{d}{dl_{2}}s_{+}(j_{l_{2}},u)|_{l_{2}=0}=-\frac{{d}{dl_{2}}r_{j_{l_{2}}}(z)|_{l_{2}=0}}{dr_{j^{\prime}}(z)\circ(d\phi_{u}(z))^{-1}(\partial_{s})}.

Since
\frac{d}{dl_{2}}r_{j_{l_{2}}}(z)|_{l_{2}=0}
depends continuously on
(j^{\prime},u)
,
ds_{+}(j^{\prime},u)(\cdot,0)
is a bounded linear map from
T_{j^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}
to
\mathbb{R}
which depends continuously on
(j^{\prime},u)
. This proves that
s_{+}
is a
C^{1}
-map when
\chi(\dot{F})<0
.

When
\chi(\dot{F})=0
or
1
,
s_{+}
is still given by Equation (
9.4.13
), but the metric
g_{(\ddot{F},j^{\prime})}
is now a function of
u
. More precisely, for any
u=(s,t,\eta)
, define

I_{u}:[R,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\quad s^{\prime}\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}((t,\eta(s,t))|_{s=s^{\prime}})

with
R\gg 0
and then

s_{+}^{\prime}(u)=I_{u}^{-1}(\varepsilon^{\prime}).

Recall that the set
\mathbf{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{2m(\gamma_{+})})
of additional punctures that we put on
\dot{F}
satisfies

q_{k}=\phi_{u}^{-1}\big(s_{+}^{\prime},\tfrac{k}{2m(\gamma_{+})}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{+})\big)\in\dot{F}.

Then
g_{(\ddot{F},j^{\prime})}
is smooth function of
\mathbf{q}
. An argument similar to that of the
\chi(\dot{F})<0
case shows that
s_{+}
is a
C^{1}
-map.
∎
